# SpaceX Starship

SpaceX Starship

Reusable superheavy-lift general-purpose launch vehicle under development

This article is about the entire launch vehicle. For the upper stage and spacecraft, see [SpaceX Starship (spacecraft)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starship_(spacecraft) "SpaceX Starship (spacecraft)").

Starship

**Starship** is a [two-stage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two-stage "Two-stage"), [fully reusable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reusable_launch_vehicle "Reusable launch vehicle"), [super heavy-lift launch vehicle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_heavy-lift_launch_vehicle "Super heavy-lift launch vehicle") under development by American aerospace company [SpaceX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX "SpaceX"). Currently built and launched from [Starbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starbase "SpaceX Starbase") in Texas, it is intended as the successor to the company's [Falcon 9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_9 "Falcon 9") and [Falcon Heavy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_Heavy "Falcon Heavy") rockets,[&#8202;](#ref-back-7-0)[\[7\]](#refnote-7) and is part of SpaceX's broader [reusable launch system development program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_reusable_launch_system_development_program "SpaceX reusable launch system development program"). If completed as designed, Starship would be the first fully reusable orbital rocket and have the [highest payload capacity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_heavy-lift_launch_vehicle#Comparison "Super heavy-lift launch vehicle") of any launch vehicle to date. As of September 28, 2026, Starship [has launched](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Starship_launches "List of Starship launches") 14 times, with 9 successful flights and 5 failures.

The vehicle consists of two stages: the [Super Heavy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Super_Heavy "SpaceX Super Heavy") booster and the [Starship spacecraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starship_(spacecraft) "SpaceX Starship (spacecraft)"), both powered by [Raptor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Raptor "SpaceX Raptor") engines burning [liquid methane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liquid_methane "Liquid methane") (the main component of [natural gas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_gas "Natural gas")) and [liquid oxygen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liquid_oxygen "Liquid oxygen"). Both stages are intended to return to the launch site and [land vertically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VTVL "VTVL") at the launch tower for potential reuse.[&#8202;](#ref-back-MH-0)[\[8\]](#refnote-MH) Once in space, the Starship upper stage is intended to function as a standalone spacecraft capable of carrying [crew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_spaceflight "Human spaceflight") and [cargo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncrewed_spacecraft "Uncrewed spacecraft").[&#8202;](#ref-back-9-0)[\[9\]](#refnote-9) Missions beyond [low Earth orbit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Low_Earth_orbit "Low Earth orbit") would require multiple in-orbit [refueling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_propellant_depot "Orbital propellant depot") flights. At the end of its mission, Starship [reenters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atmospheric_entry "Atmospheric entry") the atmosphere using [heat shield tiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atmospheric_reentry#Thermal_protection_systems "Atmospheric reentry") similar to those of the [Space Shuttle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_Shuttle "Space Shuttle").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Reichhardt-0)[\[10\]](#refnote-Reichhardt) SpaceX states that its goal is to reduce launch costs by reusing and [mass-producing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_production "Mass production") both stages.

SpaceX initially expressed ambitions to use Starship for [crewed missions to Mars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Mars_colonization_program "SpaceX Mars colonization program"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-11-0)[\[11\]](#refnote-11) but the plan was revised to prioritize returning [humans to the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_landing "Moon landing").[&#8202;](#ref-back-12-0)[\[12\]](#refnote-12) SpaceX has also proposed a wide range of missions for Starship, such as deploying large satellites, [space station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_station "Space station") modules,[&#8202;](#ref-back-space20230510-0)[\[13\]](#refnote-space20230510) and [space telescopes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_telescopes "Space telescopes").[&#8202;](#ref-back-14-0)[\[14\]](#refnote-14) A crewed variant, the [Starship Human Landing System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_HLS "Starship HLS"), is being developed under a contract with [NASA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NASA "NASA") as part of the [Artemis program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artemis_program "Artemis program"), with a crewed docking test in low Earth orbit on [Artemis III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artemis_III "Artemis III"), targeted for mid-2027, and the first crewed landing moved to [Artemis IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artemis_IV "Artemis IV"), targeted for 2028.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Ars-Berger-Artemis-0)[\[15\]](#refnote-Ars-Berger-Artemis)

SpaceX began [developing concepts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starship_design_history "SpaceX Starship design history") for a super heavy-lift reusable launch vehicle as early as 2005, though these concepts did not result in active development of Starship until 2018. Development has followed an [iterative and incremental](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iterative_and_incremental_development "Iterative and incremental development") approach, involving a high number of prototype vehicles and test flights. Due to several setbacks in development, including the loss of the first four Block 2 upper stages in 2025 (three in flight and one, Ship 36, in a ground-test explosion),[&#8202;](#ref-back-Ars-Ship36-0)[\[16\]](#refnote-Ars-Ship36) Starship did not attempt to reach orbit until its [fourteenth flight test](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_14 "Starship flight 14"), which deployed Starship's first operational payload.[&#8202;](#ref-back-17-0)[\[17\]](#refnote-17)[&#8202;](#ref-back-18-0)[\[18\]](#refnote-18)

## Description

When stacked and fully fueled, Starship has a mass of approximately 5,300t (11,700,000lb),[&#8202;](#ref-back-21-0)[\[a\]](#refnote-21) a diameter of 9m (30ft)[&#8202;](#ref-back-22-0)[\[21\]](#refnote-22) and a height of 124.4m (408ft).[&#8202;](#ref-back-Berger_blocks-0)[\[22\]](#refnote-Berger_blocks) The rocket has been designed with the goal of being fully [reusable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reusable_launch_vehicle "Reusable launch vehicle") to reduce launch costs;[&#8202;](#ref-back-Inman-0)[\[23\]](#refnote-Inman) it consists of the [Super Heavy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Super_Heavy "SpaceX Super Heavy") [booster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Booster_(rocketry) "Booster (rocketry)") and the [Starship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starship_(spacecraft) "SpaceX Starship (spacecraft)") upper stage[&#8202;](#ref-back-Amos-0)[\[24\]](#refnote-Amos) which are powered by [Raptor (RSL) and Raptor Vacuum (RVac) engines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Raptor "SpaceX Raptor").[&#8202;](#ref-back-cnet-0)[\[25\]](#refnote-cnet)

The bodies of both rocket stages are made from [stainless steel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stainless_steel "Stainless steel")[&#8202;](#ref-back-slashgear-0)[\[26\]](#refnote-slashgear) and are manufactured by stacking and welding stainless steel cylinders.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Berger-2020b-0)[\[27\]](#refnote-Berger-2020b) These cylinders have a height of 1.83m (6ft), and a thickness of 3.97mm (0.156in).[&#8202;](#ref-back-Berger-2020b-1)[\[27\]](#refnote-Berger-2020b)

### Super Heavy booster

Main article: [SpaceX Super Heavy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Super_Heavy "SpaceX Super Heavy")

Super Heavy was initially 71m (233ft) tall (in its Block 1 and Block 2 design, now retired), while Block 3 Super Heavy is 72.3 meters tall.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Berger_2024-0)[\[28\]](#refnote-Berger_2024) It is 9m (30ft) wide,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Berger_2024-1)[\[28\]](#refnote-Berger_2024) and is composed of four general sections, in ascending order: the engines, the [liquid oxygen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liquid_oxygen "Liquid oxygen") tank, the [liquid methane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liquid_methane "Liquid methane") tank, and the interstage.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Sesnic-2021sh-0)[\[29\]](#refnote-Sesnic-2021sh)

### Tanks

The two cryogenic propellant tanks on Super Heavy are separated by a common bulkhead, a similar structural design to the [S-II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S-II "S-II") and [S-IVB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S-IVB "S-IVB") stages on the [Saturn V](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saturn_V "Saturn V") rocket.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Building_SpaceX_2023-0)[\[30\]](#refnote-Building_SpaceX_2023) After [Starship's second flight test](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_2 "Starship flight test 2"), the common dome's design was changed to be more elliptical,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Everyday_Astronaut_2025-0)[\[31\]](#refnote-Everyday_Astronaut_2025) altering the propellant capacity of both tanks by a small amount.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Everyday_Astronaut_2025-1)[\[31\]](#refnote-Everyday_Astronaut_2025) Each tank possesses roughly 74 stringers for structural reinforcement, attached to their interior walls.[&#8202;](#ref-back-33-0)[\[32\]](#refnote-33) The booster's two tanks hold a combined 3,650t (8,050,000lb) of propellant:[&#8202;](#ref-back-spacex-homepage-0)[\[33\]](#refnote-spacex-homepage) 2,860t (6,310,000lb) of liquid oxygen and 790t (1,740,000lb) of liquid methane.[&#8202;](#ref-back-35-0)[\[b\]](#refnote-35) Fuel is fed to the engines via a single liquid downcomer, and channeled into distribution [manifolds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manifold_(fluid_mechanics) "Manifold (fluid mechanics)") of the engines.[&#8202;](#ref-back-36-0)[\[34\]](#refnote-36)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Building_SpaceX_2023-1)[\[30\]](#refnote-Building_SpaceX_2023)

Two quick disconnects are used for loading propellant onto the booster,[&#8202;](#ref-back-B3-0)[\[35\]](#refnote-B3) with one disconnect for liquid oxygen and the other for liquid methane.[&#8202;](#ref-back-B3-1)[\[35\]](#refnote-B3)

### Propulsion

The aft dome of the Super Heavy booster is protected by metallic heat shield tiles.[&#8202;](#ref-back-38-0)[\[36\]](#refnote-38) Additional thermal protection is present on some parts of the thrust vector control system.[&#8202;](#ref-back-39-0)[\[37\]](#refnote-39) This system uses electric actuators on the thirteen engines in the middle and center rings.[&#8202;](#ref-back-40-0)[\[38\]](#refnote-40)[&#8202;](#ref-back-spacex-ft-0)[\[39\]](#refnote-spacex-ft) Unlike the ascent and boostback burns, which use all thirty-three engines, the landing burn uses only these inner thirteen and draws fuel from dedicated header tanks.[&#8202;](#ref-back-SpaceX14-0)[\[40\]](#refnote-SpaceX14)[&#8202;](#ref-back-IS-0)[\[41\]](#refnote-IS)

The [Raptor engine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Raptor "SpaceX Raptor") uses a [full-flow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Full-flow_staged_combustion "Full-flow staged combustion") staged combustion cycle with oxygen and methane-rich turbopumps.[&#8202;](#ref-back-44-0)[\[42\]](#refnote-44)[&#8202;](#ref-back-45-0)[\[43\]](#refnote-45) Before 2014, only two full-flow staged-combustion rocket engine designs had advanced enough to undergo testing: the Soviet [RD-270](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RD-270 "RD-270") project in the 1960s and the [Aerojet Rocketdyne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aerojet_Rocketdyne "Aerojet Rocketdyne") [Integrated Powerhead Demonstrator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Integrated_Powerhead_Demonstrator "Integrated Powerhead Demonstrator") in the mid-2000s.[&#8202;](#ref-back-nsf20140307sh-0)[\[44\]](#refnote-nsf20140307sh) To improve performance, the engines burn subcooled propellant; i.e. the propellants are cooled below their respective boiling points to further increase their density and the engine mass flow rates.[&#8202;](#ref-back-spacex-homepage-1)[\[33\]](#refnote-spacex-homepage)

The Block 1 version of the booster (used through November 2024) produced a total of 73.5MN (16,500,000lbf),[&#8202;](#ref-back-musk-v-overview-0)[\[45\]](#refnote-musk-v-overview) just over twice that of the NASA [Saturn V first stage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S-IC "S-IC"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-SICMaxThrust-0)[\[46\]](#refnote-SICMaxThrust) with this total being expected to increase to 80.8MN (18,200,000lbf) for Block 3 boosters and later up to 98.1MN (22,100,000lbf) with the planned Block 4 vehicle.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Berger_2024-2)[\[28\]](#refnote-Berger_2024) The thirty-three engines collectively produce large [shock diamonds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shock_diamond "Shock diamond") in the exhaust jet, visible during ascent and descent.[&#8202;](#ref-back-49-0)[\[47\]](#refnote-49)

During unpowered flight in the upper atmosphere, [control authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spacecraft_attitude_control "Spacecraft attitude control") is provided by [cold gas thrusters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_gas_thruster "Cold gas thruster") fed with residual [ullage gas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ullage "Ullage").[&#8202;](#ref-back-RI-0)[\[48\]](#refnote-RI) Four perpendicular vents are located within the interstage.[&#8202;](#ref-back-MU-0)[\[49\]](#refnote-MU) Additional vents are located just below the common dome, pointing toward the engines at a slight angle.[&#8202;](#ref-back-MU-1)[\[49\]](#refnote-MU)

### Interstage

The interstage and forward heat shield of the super heavy booster, which enable [hot staging](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hot_staging "Hot staging"), are directly integrated into the methane tank.[&#8202;](#ref-back-B3-2)[\[35\]](#refnote-B3) During hot staging, Super Heavy shuts down all but five inner engines, and remains attached to Starship until the upper stage ignites its own engines.[&#8202;](#ref-back-SpaceX14-1)[\[40\]](#refnote-SpaceX14) Also embedded into the methane tank are three electrically actuated grid fins,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Sesnic-2021sh-1)[\[29\]](#refnote-Sesnic-2021sh)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Website-0)[\[50\]](#refnote-Website) in a 90/90/180 degree arrangement.[&#8202;](#ref-back-B3-3)[\[35\]](#refnote-B3) Two of these fins have [hardpoints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hardpoint "Hardpoint"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-Website-1)[\[50\]](#refnote-Website) which are used for lifting the vehicle onto the launch mount and for catching the booster when it lands at the launch tower.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Weber-2021sh-0)[\[51\]](#refnote-Weber-2021sh)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:0-0)[\[52\]](#refnote-:0)

Animation of Super Heavy's integration to the launch mount, using mechanical arms

### Starship spacecraft

Main article: [SpaceX Starship (spacecraft)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starship_(spacecraft) "SpaceX Starship (spacecraft)")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/9/95/Starship_internal_structure.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail_unscaled)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Starship_internal_structure.jpg) Diagram of a Block 1 Starship's internal structure. Not shown in this diagram are the flaps: the aft flaps are placed at the bottom (or left in this orientation), and the forward flaps are placed at the top (here, right) portion of the vehicle.

The now retired Block 2 version of Starship was 52.1m (171ft) tall, 9m (30ft) wide,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Berger_blocks-1)[\[22\]](#refnote-Berger_blocks) and was composed of four general sections: the engine bay, the oxygen tank, the fuel tank, and the payload bay.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Sesnic-0)[\[19\]](#refnote-Sesnic) The retired Block 1 was constructed in a similar manner, though it was only 50.3m (165ft) tall. [Elon Musk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elon_Musk "Elon Musk") stated in 2021 that the vehicle had a [dry mass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vehicle_weight "Vehicle weight") of roughly 100t (220,000lb).[&#8202;](#ref-back-Sesnic-1)[\[19\]](#refnote-Sesnic) The windward side is protected by a [heat shield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heat_shield "Heat shield") composed of eighteen thousand[&#8202;](#ref-back-Sheetz-2021a2-0)[\[53\]](#refnote-Sheetz-2021a2) hexagonal black tiles that can withstand temperatures of 1,400°C (2,600°F).[&#8202;](#ref-back-56-0)[\[54\]](#refnote-56)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Reichhardt-1)[\[10\]](#refnote-Reichhardt) It is designed to protect the vehicle during [atmospheric entry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atmospheric_entry "Atmospheric entry") and is to be used multiple times with minimal maintenance between flights.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Inman-1)[\[55\]](#refnote-Inman) The [silica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silicon_dioxide "Silicon dioxide")-based tiles are attached to Starship with pins,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Reichhardt-2)[\[10\]](#refnote-Reichhardt) and had small gaps in between to allow for [heat expansion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thermal_expansion "Thermal expansion").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Bergeron-0)[\[56\]](#refnote-Bergeron-2021) After [flight test 4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_4 "Starship flight test 4"), SpaceX added a secondary ablative layer under the primary heat shield,[&#8202;](#ref-back-59-0)[\[57\]](#refnote-59) though this was only added to the flaps of the [flight test 6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_6 "Starship flight test 6") vehicle.[&#8202;](#ref-back-60-0)[\[58\]](#refnote-60) This ablative layer is likely composed of pyron, which is similar in composition to [carbon composites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon-fiber_reinforced_polymer "Carbon-fiber reinforced polymer").[&#8202;](#ref-back-nsf-flight-0)[\[59\]](#refnote-nsf-flight) The total mass of the heat shield and ablative layer of a Block 1 ship is 10.5t (23,000lb).[&#8202;](#ref-back-62-0)[\[60\]](#refnote-62) After [flight test 10](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_10 "Starship flight test 10"), SpaceX added a felt, called "crunch wrap," between the gaps in between the tiles to prevent heat seeping in.

### Tanks

The propellant tanks on Starship are separated by a common bulkhead, similar to the ones used on the [S-II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S-II "S-II") and [S-IVB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S-IVB "S-IVB") stages on the [Saturn V](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saturn_V "Saturn V") rocket.[&#8202;](#ref-back-63-0)[\[61\]](#refnote-63) While Block 2 vehicles use an elliptical dome,[&#8202;](#ref-back-:14-0)[\[62\]](#refnote-:14) the common and forward domes of the Block 1 design were more conical.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:12-0)[\[63\]](#refnote-:12) Block 1 vehicles only had 24 [stringers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Longeron "Longeron") within the oxygen tank,[&#8202;](#ref-back-66-0)[\[64\]](#refnote-66) while Block 2 vehicles had these added to the methane tanks.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:14-1)[\[62\]](#refnote-:14) The vehicle's tanks hold 1,500t (3,300,000lb) of propellant,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Berger_blocks-2)[\[22\]](#refnote-Berger_blocks) consisting of 1,170t (2,580,000lb) of liquid oxygen and 330t (730,000lb) of liquid methane.[&#8202;](#ref-back-67-0)[\[c\]](#refnote-67)

Fuel is fed to the engines via four downcomers, with three smaller downcomers feeding the [Vacuum Raptors/RVacs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Raptor#Raptor_Vacuum "SpaceX Raptor") and the central downcomer feeding the inner three engines.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:14-2)[\[62\]](#refnote-:14) The central downcomer connects to a large sump, instead of directly to the methane tank.[&#8202;](#ref-back-68-0)[\[65\]](#refnote-68) The original design had a single downcomer.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:10-0)[\[66\]](#refnote-:10) The liquid oxygen (LOX) downcomer extends into the LOX tank, with a small expanded portion of unknown purpose.[&#8202;](#ref-back-70-0)[\[67\]](#refnote-70) Two additional downcomers route methane and oxygen from the header tanks.[&#8202;](#ref-back-71-0)[\[68\]](#refnote-71) A camera is located on the walls of the tank, pointed towards the payload bay.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:09-0)[\[69\]](#refnote-:09)

The oxygen tank terminates with the thrust structure of the vehicle.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:12-1)[\[63\]](#refnote-:12) The RVac engines are mounted directly to the aft dome, which has reinforcements mounted inside of the tank.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:12-2)[\[63\]](#refnote-:12) The three sea level engines are mounted on the thrust puck, which forms the bottom of the aft dome.[&#8202;](#ref-back-73-0)[\[70\]](#refnote-73) A conical steel structure is mounted inside the bottom of the dome, reinforcing the thrust puck enough to enable its support of the inner three engines.[&#8202;](#ref-back-74-0)[\[71\]](#refnote-74) The propellant lines on the vehicles are [vacuum jacketed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multi-layer_insulation "Multi-layer insulation"), reducing boiloff while in orbit.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:10-1)[\[66\]](#refnote-:10)

### Propulsion

Starship is powered by six [Raptor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Raptor "SpaceX Raptor") engines, which are housed within a dedicated shielding compartment.[&#8202;](#ref-back-75-0)[\[72\]](#refnote-75) Blocks 1 through 3 have three sea-level engines, as well as three engines optimized for operation in the vacuum of space, called RVacs.[&#8202;](#ref-back-nsf-block-0)[\[73\]](#refnote-nsf-block) Block 4 ships are expected to have three additional RVacs.[&#8202;](#ref-back-nsf-block-1)[\[73\]](#refnote-nsf-block) The sea-level engines are equipped with [gimbal actuators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gimbaled_thrust "Gimbaled thrust"), and reignite for the landing burns.[&#8202;](#ref-back-nsf-flight-1)[\[59\]](#refnote-nsf-flight) After [Starship's second flight test](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_2 "Starship flight test 2"), this gimbaling system was switched from a hydraulic system to an electric one, enabling the removal of the hydraulic power units.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Spacex202502-0)[\[74\]](#refnote-Spacex202502) This change was made to the booster after the [first flight test](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_1 "Starship flight test 1").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Spacex202502-1)[\[74\]](#refnote-Spacex202502) There are four engine chill lines on board the vehicle, though two of these lines may serve another purpose.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:16-0)[\[75\]](#refnote-:16)

Each engine is protected by a dedicated shielding compartment.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:16-1)[\[75\]](#refnote-:16) Beginning with S25, the Block 1 design had between 14 and 16 such vents.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:16-2)[\[75\]](#refnote-:16) Additional vents were added after flight 7.[&#8202;](#ref-back-79-0)[\[76\]](#refnote-79) The [fire suppression system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vehicle_fire_suppression_system "Vehicle fire suppression system"), which uses gaseous nitrogen to purge the engine bay during flight, was upgraded after flight 7.[&#8202;](#ref-back-80-0)[\[77\]](#refnote-80) A similar system on the booster uses carbon dioxide to purge the individual engine compartments during flight and static fires.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Spacex202502-2)[\[74\]](#refnote-Spacex202502)

The Raptor engine uses a [full-flow staged combustion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Full-flow_staged_combustion "Full-flow staged combustion") cycle, which has oxygen and methane-rich turbopumps.[&#8202;](#ref-back-81-0)[\[78\]](#refnote-81) Before 2014, only two full-flow staged-combustion rocket engine designs had advanced enough to undergo testing: the Soviet [RD-270](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RD-270 "RD-270") project in the 1960s and the Aerojet Rocketdyne [Integrated Powerhead Demonstrator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Integrated_Powerhead_Demonstrator "Integrated Powerhead Demonstrator") in the mid-2000s.[&#8202;](#ref-back-nsf20140307-0)[\[79\]](#refnote-nsf20140307) To improve performance, the engines burn [supercooled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supercooling "Supercooling") propellant.[&#8202;](#ref-back-StarshipHomepage2-0)[\[80\]](#refnote-StarshipHomepage2)

The Block 1 version of the ship (used through November 2024) produced a total of 12.25MN (2,750,000lbf)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Berger_blocks-3)[\[22\]](#refnote-Berger_blocks) almost triple the thrust of the [Saturn V second stage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S-II "S-II"), with this being expected to increase to 15.69MN (3,530,000lbf) for Block 2 boosters and later up to 26.48MN (5,950,000lbf) with the Block 3 vehicle.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Berger_blocks-4)[\[22\]](#refnote-Berger_blocks)

During unpowered flight in orbit, [control authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spacecraft_attitude_control "Spacecraft attitude control") is provided by [cold gas thrusters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_gas_thruster "Cold gas thruster") fed with residual [ullage gas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ullage "Ullage").[&#8202;](#ref-back-:12-3)[\[63\]](#refnote-:12) Four such thrusters are located just below the payload bay,[&#8202;](#ref-back-84-0)[\[81\]](#refnote-84) and two on the oxygen tank.[&#8202;](#ref-back-nsf20220331-0)[\[82\]](#refnote-nsf20220331) Near the top of the nosecone, there are two vents connected to the header tanks.[&#8202;](#ref-back-nsf20220331-1)[\[82\]](#refnote-nsf20220331) Additional vents were added at the base of the vehicle after flight two.[&#8202;](#ref-back-86-0)[\[83\]](#refnote-86)

### Payload bay

The payload bay hosts the nosecone, header tanks, forward flaps, multiple COPVs, and the so-called " [PEZ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PEZ "PEZ") dispenser".[&#8202;](#ref-back-NASASpaceflight2025-0)[\[84\]](#refnote-NASASpaceflight2025) The header tanks are mounted at the tip of the payload bay.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:12-4)[\[63\]](#refnote-:12) The LOX header tank forms the top of the nosecone, with the methane header tank attached directly below it.[&#8202;](#ref-back-nsf-manufacturing-0)[\[85\]](#refnote-nsf-manufacturing) These tanks terminate in a conical sump, which are attached to the downcomers.[&#8202;](#ref-back-89-0)[\[86\]](#refnote-89) Several COPVs are mounted in the space around the methane header tank, providing the startup gas for the engines,[&#8202;](#ref-back-NASASpaceflight2025-1)[\[84\]](#refnote-NASASpaceflight2025) with twelve additional COPVs within the base of the payload bay.[&#8202;](#ref-back-90-0)[\[87\]](#refnote-90)

The nosecone has substantial internal reinforcement, mainly around the forward flap attachment points and lifting points for the chopsticks.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NASASpaceflight2025-2)[\[84\]](#refnote-NASASpaceflight2025) The number of internal stringers was increased between Block 1 and Block 2 vehicles.[&#8202;](#ref-back-rw-s33-nose-0)[\[88\]](#refnote-rw-s33-nose) Additional reinforcements are used to support the PEZ dispenser on ships equipped with one.[&#8202;](#ref-back-rw-s33-nose-1)[\[88\]](#refnote-rw-s33-nose) Four Starlink antennas are located within the nosecone.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:26-0)[\[89\]](#refnote-:26)

The PEZ dispenser is used to deploy Starlink satellites into LEO.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:12-5)[\[63\]](#refnote-:12) It was first added to S24, though it was permanently sealed until [flight 3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_3 "Starship flight test 3"). It consists of the dispenser mechanism and the door.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:12-6)[\[63\]](#refnote-:12) The door opens by folding into the payload bay.[&#8202;](#ref-back-93-0)[\[90\]](#refnote-93)

The dispenser itself is mounted directly to the forward dome.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:52-0)[\[91\]](#refnote-:52) It has a truss structure for its base, with solid steel used elsewhere.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:52-1)[\[91\]](#refnote-:52) A mobile track is used in the base, enabling the dispenser to push the satellite out of the vehicle.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:52-2)[\[91\]](#refnote-:52) After dispensing a satellite, the next payload is lowered onto the base, and is deployed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:52-3)[\[91\]](#refnote-:52) The opposite occurs during loading, with the dispenser raising its payloads to receive another satellite.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:52-4)[\[91\]](#refnote-:52) In order to prevent the satellite from floating out of the mechanism during zero-g operations, the dispenser locks the satellites in position using a "retention frame". This is lowered alongside the satellites during operation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:52-5)[\[91\]](#refnote-:52)

### Flaps

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/11/Starship_SN15_flap_and_nosecone_%2851437260707%29.jpg/500px-Starship_SN15_flap_and_nosecone_%2851437260707%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Starship_SN15_flap_and_nosecone_(51437260707).jpg) Forward flap on a suborbital Starship prototype

Starship controls its reentry with four flaps, two aft flaps mounted to the sides of the engine bay and LOX tank and two forward flaps on the payload bay.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:11-0)[\[92\]](#refnote-:11) Substantial reinforcements are present in the nosecone for the support of the forward flaps.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:14-3)[\[62\]](#refnote-:14) According to SpaceX, the flaps replace the need for wings or [tailplane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tailplane "Tailplane"), reduce the fuel needed for landing, and allow landing at destinations in the [Solar System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_System "Solar System") where runways do not exist (for example, Mars). The flap's hinges are sealed in [aerocovers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aeroshell "Aeroshell") because they would otherwise be easily damaged during reentry.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Sesnic-2)[\[19\]](#refnote-Sesnic) Static wicks are present on the flaps, aiding in the discharge of static electricity.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:26-1)[\[89\]](#refnote-:26)

Despite this, damage to the forward flaps was observed on flights [four](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_4 "Starship flight test 4"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-:11-1)[\[92\]](#refnote-:11) [five](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_5 "Starship flight test 5"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-nsf-flight-2)[\[59\]](#refnote-nsf-flight) and [six](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_6 "Starship flight test 6"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-96-0)[\[93\]](#refnote-96) with near complete loss occurring on flight 4.[&#8202;](#ref-back-97-0)[\[94\]](#refnote-97) Beginning with Block 2, the design of these forward flaps was significantly changed, moving leeward and becoming thinner and angled.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:09-1)[\[69\]](#refnote-:09) This sets them at an approximately 140-degree angle, compared to the 180-degree angle of the aft flaps and previous version of the forward flaps.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:10-2)[\[66\]](#refnote-:10) This change was made to prevent the static aero from creating a tendency for the Ship to pitch up, even when the forward flaps were stowed,[&#8202;](#ref-back-98-0)[\[95\]](#refnote-98) and also reduces the heating on the static aero and forward flaps observed on the last three flights of the Block 1 ship.[&#8202;](#ref-back-eda20240622-0)[\[96\]](#refnote-eda20240622) Both sets of flaps feature cameras in their hinges.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:09-2)[\[69\]](#refnote-:09)

### Raptor engine

Main article: [SpaceX Raptor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Raptor "SpaceX Raptor")

[![A rocket engine with nozzle and intricate plumbing](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c3/SpaceX_sea-level_Raptor_at_Hawthorne_-_1.jpg/500px-SpaceX_sea-level_Raptor_at_Hawthorne_-_1.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:SpaceX_sea-level_Raptor_at_Hawthorne_-_1.jpg) Sea level-optimized Raptor 1 engine, May 2020

Raptor is a family of rocket engines developed by SpaceX for use in [Starship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starship_(spacecraft) "SpaceX Starship (spacecraft)") and [Super Heavy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Heavy_(booster_rocket) "Super Heavy (booster rocket)") vehicles. It burns [liquid oxygen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liquid_oxygen "Liquid oxygen") and [methane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Methane "Methane") in an efficient and complex [full-flow staged combustion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Full-flow_staged_combustion "Full-flow staged combustion") power cycle. The Raptor engine uses methane as fuel rather than kerosene because methane gives higher performance and prevents the build-up of deposits in the engine from [coking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coking "Coking").[&#8202;](#ref-back-O'Callaghan-0)[\[97\]](#refnote-O'Callaghan) Methane can also be produced from carbon dioxide and hydrogen using the [Sabatier reaction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabatier_reaction "Sabatier reaction").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Sommerlad-0)[\[98\]](#refnote-Sommerlad) The engines are designed to be reused many times with little maintenance.[&#8202;](#ref-back-102-0)[\[99\]](#refnote-102)

Raptor operates with an [oxygen-to-methane mixture ratio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rocket_propellant#Mixture_ratio "Rocket propellant") of about 3.6:1, lower than the [stoichiometric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stoichiometry "Stoichiometry") mixture ratio of 4:1 necessary for complete combustion, since operating at higher temperatures would melt the engine.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Sesnic-3)[\[19\]](#refnote-Sesnic) The propellants leave the [pre-burners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Preburner "Preburner") and get injected into the main combustion chamber as hot gases instead of liquid droplets, enabling a higher [power density](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Power_density "Power density") as the propellants mix rapidly via [diffusion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molecular_diffusion "Molecular diffusion").[&#8202;](#ref-back-O'Callaghan-1)[\[97\]](#refnote-O'Callaghan) The methane and oxygen are at high enough temperatures and pressures that they [ignite on contact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypergolic_propellant "Hypergolic propellant"), eliminating the need for igniters in the main combustion chamber.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Sesnic-4)[\[100\]](#refnote-Sesnic) The engine structure itself is mostly aluminum, copper, and steel; oxidizer-side turbopumps and manifolds subject to corrosive oxygen-rich flames are made of an [Inconel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inconel "Inconel")-like [SX500 superalloy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SX500_(superalloy) "SX500 (superalloy)").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Sesnic-5)[\[100\]](#refnote-Sesnic) Some components are [3D printed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/3D-printed "3D-printed").[&#8202;](#ref-back-104-0)[\[101\]](#refnote-104)

A Raptor 2 engine produces 2.3 [MN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newton_(unit) "Newton (unit)") (520,000lbf) at a [specific impulse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Specific_impulse "Specific impulse") of 327 seconds (3.21km/s) at [sea level](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sea_level "Sea level") and 350 seconds (3.4km/s) in a vacuum.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Sesnic-6)[\[100\]](#refnote-Sesnic) [Raptor vacuum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raptor_vacuum "Raptor vacuum"), used on the Starship upper stage, is modified with a regeneratively cooled [nozzle extension](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nozzle_extension "Nozzle extension") made of brazed steel tubes, increasing its expansion ratio to about 90 and its specific impulse in vacuum to 380 seconds (3.7km/s).[&#8202;](#ref-back-Sesnic-7)[\[19\]](#refnote-Sesnic) The main [combustion chamber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Combustion_chamber "Combustion chamber") operates at a pressure of 350bar (5,100psi) exceeding that of any prior operational rocket engine.[&#8202;](#ref-back-O'Callaghan-2)[\[97\]](#refnote-O'Callaghan) The Raptor's [gimbaling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gimbal "Gimbal") range is 15°, higher than the [RS-25](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RS-25 "RS-25")'s 12.5° and the Merlin's 5°. SpaceX has stated they aim to achieve a per unit production cost of US$250,000 upon starting mass production.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Sesnic-8)[\[100\]](#refnote-Sesnic)

| Ship propellant (t) | 1,200 | 1,500 | 1,600 | 2,300 |
| Booster liftoff thrust (tf) | 7,500[&#8202;](#ref-back-ift6-musk-0)[\[103\]](#refnote-ift6-musk) | 8,240 | 10,000 |  |
