Performance metrics[&#8202;](#ref-back-Berger_blocks-5)[\[22\]](#refnote-Berger_blocks)[&#8202;](#ref-back-105-0)[\[102\]](#refnote-105)

On April 4, 2024, [Elon Musk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elon_Musk "Elon Musk") provided an update on Starship at [Starbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starbase "SpaceX Starbase"), where two new versions of Starship were announced, Block 2 and Block 3,[&#8202;](#ref-back-107-0)[\[104\]](#refnote-107) intended to address the shortcomings of the pre-production prototypes.

### Block 1

Block 1 was used after [2020–2021 flight tests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Starship_upper_stage_flight_tests "List of Starship upper stage flight tests"), beginning with [flight test 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_1 "Starship flight test 1") at the start of 2023, to [flight tests 5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_5 "Starship flight test 5") and [6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_6 "Starship flight test 6"), then retired.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Clark_Block_2-0)[\[105\]](#refnote-Clark_Block_2)

### Block 2

Block 2 for both stages was used beginning with [flight test 7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_7 "Starship flight test 7") at the start of 2025.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Clark_Block_2-1)[\[105\]](#refnote-Clark_Block_2) Block 2 upper stage vehicles featured a thinner forward flap design, flaps that are positioned more leeward, a 25% increase in propellant capacity, redesigned avionics.[&#8202;](#ref-back-109-0)[\[106\]](#refnote-109) The integrated vehicle is 3.1m (10ft) taller than the previous Block 1 vehicle.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Clark_Block_2-2)[\[105\]](#refnote-Clark_Block_2) Block 2 was retired without attempting an orbital flight. Additionally, Block 2 vehicles were planned to use Raptor 3 engines, removing the need for secondary engine shielding.[&#8202;](#ref-back-110-0)[\[107\]](#refnote-110) The Block 2 ship and booster first flew on flight test 7.[&#8202;](#ref-back-111-0)[\[108\]](#refnote-111) After [flight test 11](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_11 "Starship flight test 11"), Block 2 was retired.

### Block 3

Block 3 ships have [Raptor 3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Raptor#Raptor_3 "SpaceX Raptor") engines,[&#8202;](#ref-back-FS-0)[\[109\]](#refnote-FS) as well as hardware for refueling operations in LEO, such as docking ports and a redesigned quick disconnect.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FS-1)[\[109\]](#refnote-FS) The switch to Raptor 3 also enables the removal of most of the aft section's shielding.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FS-2)[\[109\]](#refnote-FS)

Block 3 boosters have an integrated vented interstage/forward dome, three grid fins instead of the prior four, and use the grid fins as tower catch points, eliminating the need for separate catch point structure.[&#8202;](#ref-back-B3-4)[\[35\]](#refnote-B3) Like with the ship, the Block 3 boosters use Raptor 3 engines, allowing for the removal of the majority of the booster's engine shielding, reducing weight by over a ton per engine.[&#8202;](#ref-back-B3-5)[\[35\]](#refnote-B3)
Block 3 (also called V3) first flew on [flight test 12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_12 "Starship flight test 12") on May 22, 2026, which was also the first flight of Raptor 3 engines and the first launch from Starbase's second pad.[&#8202;](#ref-back-SpaceX-F12-0)[\[110\]](#refnote-SpaceX-F12)

## Planned launch and landing profile

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e9/Starship_Booster_Return_on_Final_Approach_%2854063904149%29.jpg/500px-Starship_Booster_Return_on_Final_Approach_%2854063904149%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Starship_Booster_Return_on_Final_Approach_(54063904149).jpg) Super Heavy Booster 12 approaching the tower during [Starship flight test 5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_5 "Starship flight test 5") on October 13, 2024

Payloads will be integrated into Starship at a separate facility and then rolled out to the [launch site](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spaceport "Spaceport").[&#8202;](#ref-back-FAA-0)[\[111\]](#refnote-FAA) [Super Heavy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Super_Heavy "SpaceX Super Heavy") and [Starship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starship_(spacecraft) "SpaceX Starship (spacecraft)") are then to be stacked onto their launch mount and loaded with fuel via the ship quick disconnect (SQD) arm and booster quick disconnect (BQD).[&#8202;](#ref-back-Weber-0)[\[112\]](#refnote-Weber) The SQD and BQD retract, all 33 engines of Super Heavy ignite, and the rocket lifts off.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Weber-1)[\[112\]](#refnote-Weber)

At approximately 141 seconds after launch[&#8202;](#ref-back-Moon-0)[\[113\]](#refnote-Moon) at an altitude of roughly 64km (40mi), Super Heavy cuts off 28 of its 33 engines, leaving 5 engines running at reduced throttle.[&#8202;](#ref-back-117-0)[\[114\]](#refnote-117):58 Starship then ignites its engines while still attached to the booster, and separates.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Skibba-0)[\[115\]](#refnote-Skibba) The booster then rotates to an aft-first attitude, before relighting the 28 other engines for a "boostback burn"[&#8202;](#ref-back-SpaceX-2023b-0)[\[116\]](#refnote-SpaceX-2023b) which stops all forward velocity, then switching to 13 engines and finally 3 to precisely target the landing area. After the boostback burn, the booster's engines shut off with Super Heavy on a trajectory for a controlled descent to the launch site using its three grid fins for minor course corrections. Roughly six minutes after launch, shortly before landing,[&#8202;](#ref-back-120-0)[\[117\]](#refnote-120) it ignites its inner 13 engines, then shuts off all but the same 5 used for hot-staging,[&#8202;](#ref-back-SpaceX-2024a-0)[\[118\]](#refnote-SpaceX-2024a) to perform a [landing burn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VTVL "VTVL") which slows it sufficiently to be finally caught by a pair of [electric actuating arms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Actuator#Electric "Actuator") attached to the launch tower.[&#8202;](#ref-back-MH-1)[\[8\]](#refnote-MH) The booster landing and catch was successfully demonstrated for the first time on October 13, 2024, with the landing of Booster 12, and twice more with Boosters 14 and 15.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Clark-catch-0)[\[119\]](#refnote-Clark-catch)

Meanwhile, the Starship spacecraft continues accelerating to orbital velocity with its six Raptor engines.[&#8202;](#ref-back-123-0)[\[120\]](#refnote-123) Once in orbit, the spacecraft is planned to be able to be refueled by another Starship tanker variant.[&#8202;](#ref-back-O'Callaghan-3)[\[121\]](#refnote-O'Callaghan) Musk has estimated that 8 launches would be needed to refuel a Starship in low Earth orbit completely.[&#8202;](#ref-back-125-0)[\[122\]](#refnote-125) A NASA official estimated in 2023 that a Starship lunar landing would require launches "in the high teens", made in short succession because of cryogenic propellant boil-off.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Foust-0)[\[123\]](#refnote-Foust) To land on bodies without an atmosphere, such as the Moon, Starship will fire its engines to slow down.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Foust-1)[\[124\]](#refnote-Foust) To land on bodies with an atmosphere, such as the Earth and Mars, Starship first slows by entering the atmosphere using a [heat shield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heat_shield "Heat shield").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Inman-2)[\[23\]](#refnote-Inman) The spacecraft would then perform a "belly-flop" maneuver by diving through the atmosphere at a 60° angle to the ground,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Chang-2019b-0)[\[125\]](#refnote-Chang-2019b) controlling its fall using four flaps at the front and aft of the spacecraft.[&#8202;](#ref-back-20201210cnn-tech-0)[\[126\]](#refnote-20201210cnn-tech) Shortly before landing, the Raptor engines fire,[&#8202;](#ref-back-20201210cnn-tech-1)[\[126\]](#refnote-20201210cnn-tech) using fuel from the header tanks,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Kooser-0)[\[127\]](#refnote-Kooser) to perform a "landing flip" maneuver to return to a vertical orientation, with the Raptor engines' gimbaling helping to maneuver the craft.[&#8202;](#ref-back-20201210cnn-tech-2)[\[126\]](#refnote-20201210cnn-tech) Omitting the [thermal protection system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atmospheric_entry "Atmospheric entry"), flaps, and tower-catch hardware for a guided atmospheric deceleration and "Mechazilla" tower catch allows for significant weight savings on the HLS and depot.[&#8202;](#ref-back-131-0)[\[128\]](#refnote-131)

If Starship's second stage lands on a pad, a mobile hydraulic lift will move it to a transporter vehicle. If it lands on a [floating platform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomous_spaceport_drone_ship "Autonomous spaceport drone ship"), it will be transported by a [barge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barge "Barge") to a port and then transported by road. The recovered Starship will either be positioned on the launch mount for another launch or refurbished at a [SpaceX facility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_facilities "SpaceX facilities").[&#8202;](#ref-back-FAA-1)[\[111\]](#refnote-FAA):22

## Development

### Early design concepts (2012–2019)

Main article: [SpaceX Starship design history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starship_design_history "SpaceX Starship design history")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/58/SpaceX_CEO_Elon_Musk_visits_N%26NC_and_AFSPC_%28190416-F-ZZ999-006%29_%28cropped%29.jpg/500px-SpaceX_CEO_Elon_Musk_visits_N%26NC_and_AFSPC_%28190416-F-ZZ999-006%29_%28cropped%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:SpaceX_CEO_Elon_Musk_visits_N&NC_and_AFSPC_(190416-F-ZZ999-006)_(cropped).jpg) SpaceX CEO [Elon Musk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elon_Musk "Elon Musk") holding a model of BFR (Prior name of upper stage of Starship vehicle)

In November 2005,[&#8202;](#ref-back-tsr20051114-0)[\[129\]](#refnote-tsr20051114) before SpaceX had launched its first rocket the [Falcon 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_1 "Falcon 1"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-133-0)[\[130\]](#refnote-133) CEO [Elon Musk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elon_Musk "Elon Musk") first mentioned a high-capacity rocket concept able to launch 100t (220,000lb) to [low Earth orbit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Low_Earth_orbit "Low Earth orbit"), dubbed the _BFR_.[&#8202;](#ref-back-tsr20051114-1)[\[129\]](#refnote-tsr20051114) Later in 2012, Elon Musk first publicly announced plans to develop a rocket surpassing the capabilities of Space X's existing [Falcon 9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_9 "Falcon 9").[&#8202;](#ref-back-fg201210152-0)[\[131\]](#refnote-fg201210152) SpaceX called it the _[Mars Colonial Transporter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mars_Colonial_Transporter "Mars Colonial Transporter")_, as the rocket was to transport humans to Mars and back.[&#8202;](#ref-back-nsf20140307-1)[\[79\]](#refnote-nsf20140307) In 2016, the descriptor was changed to _[Interplanetary Transport System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interplanetary_Transport_System "Interplanetary Transport System")_, as the rocket was planned to travel beyond Mars as well.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ars20160918-0)[\[132\]](#refnote-ars20160918) The conceptual design called for a [carbon fiber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_fiber "Carbon fiber") structure,[&#8202;](#ref-back-nsf20160927a-0)[\[133\]](#refnote-nsf20160927a) a mass in excess of 10,000t (22,000,000lb) when fully fueled, a payload of 300t (660,000lb) to low Earth orbit while being fully reusable.[&#8202;](#ref-back-nsf20160927a-1)[\[133\]](#refnote-nsf20160927a) By 2017, the concept was again re-dubbed the _[BFR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Falcon_Rocket "Big Falcon Rocket")_.[&#8202;](#ref-back-musk20170929-0)[\[134\]](#refnote-musk20170929)

In December 2018, the structural material was changed from carbon composites[&#8202;](#ref-back-nsf20160927a-2)[\[133\]](#refnote-nsf20160927a) to stainless steel,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Foust-2)[\[135\]](#refnote-Foust) marking the transition from early design concepts of the Starship.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Chang-2019b-1)[\[125\]](#refnote-Chang-2019b) Musk cited numerous reasons for the change of material; low cost and ease of manufacture, increased strength of stainless steel at [cryogenic temperatures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cryogenic_rocket_engine "Cryogenic rocket engine"), as well as its ability to withstand high heat.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Chang-2019b-2)[\[125\]](#refnote-Chang-2019b) In 2019, SpaceX began to refer to the entire vehicle as Starship, with the second stage also being called [Starship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starship_(spacecraft) "SpaceX Starship (spacecraft)"), and the booster [Super Heavy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Heavy_(rocket_stage) "Super Heavy (rocket stage)").[&#8202;](#ref-back-ars20190929-0)[\[136\]](#refnote-ars20190929) They also announced that Starship would use reusable heat-shield tiles similar to [those of the Space Shuttle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_Shuttle_thermal_protection_system "Space Shuttle thermal protection system").[&#8202;](#ref-back-140-0)[\[137\]](#refnote-140) The second-stage design had also settled on six Raptor engines by 2019: three [optimized for sea-level](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rocket_engine_nozzle#Atmospheric_use "Rocket engine nozzle") and three [optimized for vacuum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rocket_engine_nozzle#Vacuum_use "Rocket engine nozzle").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Pappalardo-0)[\[138\]](#refnote-Pappalardo) In 2019, SpaceX announced a change to the second stage's design, reducing the number of aft flaps from three to two to reduce weight.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Foust-3)[\[139\]](#refnote-Foust) In March 2020 SpaceX released a Starship Users Guide, in which they stated the payload of Starship to Low Earth Orbit (LEO) would be over 100t (220,000lb), with a payload to GTO of 21t (46,000lb).[&#8202;](#ref-back-SpaceX-users-guide-0)[\[140\]](#refnote-SpaceX-users-guide)

### Low-altitude flight tests (2019–2021)

See also: [List of Starship upper stage flight tests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Starship_upper_stage_flight_tests "List of Starship upper stage flight tests")

#### _Starhopper_ to SN6

Further information: [List of Starship vehicles §Hops (SN3–SN6)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Starship_vehicles#Hops_(SN3%E2%80%93SN6) "List of Starship vehicles")

[![Short steel rocket with its fins touching the ground](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/da/SpaceX_Starhopper.jpg/500px-SpaceX_Starhopper.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:SpaceX_Starhopper.jpg)

Starhopper under construction, March 2019

[![Crane hooking onto a steel vessel body](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e3/Starship_sn5.jpg/500px-Starship_sn5.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Starship_sn5.jpg)

A crane lifting Starship SN5, August 2020

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0c/Boca_Fuego%21_The_Raptor_Engine_Test_on_the_SpaceX_Starship_SN4_%2849861436766%29.jpg/500px-Boca_Fuego%21_The_Raptor_Engine_Test_on_the_SpaceX_Starship_SN4_%2849861436766%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Boca_Fuego!_The_Raptor_Engine_Test_on_the_SpaceX_Starship_SN4_(49861436766).jpg) Static fire of SN4

The first tests started with the construction of the first prototype in 2018, _[Starhopper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starhopper "Starhopper")_, which performed several [static fires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Static_fire "Static fire") and two successful low-altitude flights in 2019.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Harwood-0)[\[141\]](#refnote-Harwood) SpaceX began constructing the first full-size [Starship MK1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_MK1 "Starship MK1") and [MK2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_MK2 "Starship MK2") upper-stage prototypes before 2019, at the SpaceX facilities in [Boca Chica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boca_Chica_(Texas) "Boca Chica (Texas)"), [Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas "Texas"), and [Cocoa, Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cocoa,_Florida "Cocoa, Florida"), respectively.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Ryan-0)[\[142\]](#refnote-Ryan) Neither prototype flew: MK1 was destroyed in November 2019 during a pressure [stress test](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stress_testing "Stress testing") and MK2's Florida facility was deconstructed throughout 2020.[&#8202;](#ref-back-146-0)[\[143\]](#refnote-146) Prototypes were built using [301 stainless steel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SAE_steel_grades#300_Series%E2%80%94austenitic_chromium-nickel_alloys "SAE steel grades").[&#8202;](#ref-back-147-0)[\[144\]](#refnote-147) This was noted for its corrosion resistance and lower cost compared to carbon fiber but faced some challenges, particularly with interlaminar toughness at cryogenic temperatures.

SpaceX then began naming its new Starship upper-stage prototypes with the prefix "SN", short for " [serial number](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serial_number "Serial number")".[&#8202;](#ref-back-Berger-2020a-0)[\[145\]](#refnote-Berger-2020a) No prototypes between [SN1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_SN1 "Starship SN1") and [SN4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_SN4 "Starship SN4") flew either—SN1 and [SN3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_SN3 "Starship SN3") collapsed during pressure stress tests, and SN4 exploded after its fifth engine firing.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NSF12-0)[\[146\]](#refnote-NSF12)

In June 2020, SpaceX started constructing a launch pad for orbital Starship flights.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Weber-2)[\[112\]](#refnote-Weber) The first flight-capable prototype, [SN5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_SN5 "Starship SN5"), was [cylindrical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cylinder "Cylinder") as it had no flaps or nose cone: just one Raptor engine, fuel tanks, and a [mass simulator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boilerplate_(spaceflight) "Boilerplate (spaceflight)").[&#8202;](#ref-back-150-0)[\[147\]](#refnote-150) On August 5, 2020, SN5 performed a 150m (500ft) high flight and successfully landed on a nearby pad.[&#8202;](#ref-back-151-0)[\[148\]](#refnote-151) On September 3, 2020, the similar-looking [Starship SN6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_SN6 "Starship SN6") repeated the hop;[&#8202;](#ref-back-152-0)[\[149\]](#refnote-152) later that month, a [Raptor vacuum engine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raptor_vacuum_engine "Raptor vacuum engine") underwent its first full duration firing at [McGregor, Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McGregor,_Texas "McGregor, Texas").[&#8202;](#ref-back-153-0)[\[150\]](#refnote-153)

#### SN8 to SN15

Further information: [List of Starship vehicles §High-altitude test flights (SN8–SN15)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Starship_vehicles#High-altitude_test_flights_(SN8%E2%80%93SN15) "List of Starship vehicles")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/SpaceX_Starship_SN8_launch_as_viewed_from_South_Padre_Island_%28cropped%29.jpg/500px-SpaceX_Starship_SN8_launch_as_viewed_from_South_Padre_Island_%28cropped%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:SpaceX_Starship_SN8_launch_as_viewed_from_South_Padre_Island_(cropped).jpg) SN8 shortly after taking off, December 2020[![Photograph of Elon Musk alongside the remains of Starship](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c8/Elon_%26_The_Starship_%2850710186528%29.jpg/500px-Elon_%26_The_Starship_%2850710186528%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Elon_&_The_Starship_(50710186528).jpg) SN8 remains after hard landing and subsequent explosionComputer animation depicting a successful high-altitude flight test such as SN10 and SN15

[Starship SN8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_SN8 "Starship SN8") was the first full-sized upper-stage prototype, though it lacked a heat shield.[&#8202;](#ref-back-154-0)[\[151\]](#refnote-154) It underwent four preliminary static fire tests between October and November 2020.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NSF12-1)[\[146\]](#refnote-NSF12) On December 9, 2020, SN8 flew, slowly turning off its three engines one by one, and reached an altitude of 12.5km (7.8mi). After SN8 dove back to the ground, its engines were hampered by low methane header tank pressure during the landing attempt, which led to a hard impact on the landing pad and subsequent explosion of the vehicle.[&#8202;](#ref-back-20201210cnn-tech-3)[\[126\]](#refnote-20201210cnn-tech) SN7 used [304L stainless steel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SAE_304_stainless_steel "SAE 304 stainless steel"), which is less brittle and more weldable.[&#8202;](#ref-back-155-0)[\[152\]](#refnote-155) Later vehicles used a proprietary alloy, 30X, whose composition is proprietary that costs slightly over €3.6/kg.[&#8202;](#ref-back-156-0)[\[153\]](#refnote-156)

Because SpaceX had violated its [launch license](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Launch_license "Launch license") and ignored warnings of worsening [shock wave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shock_wave "Shock wave") damage, the [Federal Aviation Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Aviation_Administration "Federal Aviation Administration") investigated the incident for two months.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Roulette-0)[\[154\]](#refnote-Roulette) During the SN8 launch, SpaceX ignored FAA warnings that the flight profile posed a risk of explosion.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Roulette-1)[\[154\]](#refnote-Roulette) FAA space division chief [Wayne Monteith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayne_Monteith "Wayne Monteith") said SpaceX's violation was "inconsistent with a strong safety culture", and criticized the company for proceeding with the launch "based on 'impressions' and 'assumptions,' rather than procedural checks and positive affirmations".[&#8202;](#ref-back-Roulette-2)[\[154\]](#refnote-Roulette)

On February 2, 2021, [Starship SN9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_SN9 "Starship SN9") launched to 10km (6.2mi) in a flight path similar to SN8. The prototype crashed upon landing because one engine did not ignite properly.[&#8202;](#ref-back-158-0)[\[155\]](#refnote-158) A month later, on March 3, [Starship SN10](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_SN10 "Starship SN10") launched on the same flight path as SN9.[&#8202;](#ref-back-159-0)[\[156\]](#refnote-159) The vehicle landed hard and crushed its landing legs, leaning to one side.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Chang-0)[\[157\]](#refnote-Chang) A fire was seen at the vehicle's base and it exploded less than ten minutes later,[&#8202;](#ref-back-FoustSurvives-0)[\[158\]](#refnote-FoustSurvives) potentially due to a propellant tank rupture.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Chang-1)[\[157\]](#refnote-Chang) On March 30, [Starship SN11](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_SN11 "Starship SN11") flew into thick fog along the same flight path.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Mack-2021a-0)[\[159\]](#refnote-Mack-2021a) The vehicle exploded during descent,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Mack-2021a-1)[\[159\]](#refnote-Mack-2021a) possibly due to excess propellant in a Raptor's methane turbopump.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Foust-4)[\[160\]](#refnote-Foust-2021)

In March 2021, the company disclosed a public construction plan for two [sub-orbital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sub-orbital_spaceflight "Sub-orbital spaceflight") launch pads, two orbital launch pads, two landing pads, two test stands, and a large propellant tank farm.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ars-0)[\[161\]](#refnote-ars) The company soon proposed developing the surrounding [Boca Chica Village, Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boca_Chica_Village "Boca Chica Village"), into a [company town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Company_town "Company town") named [Starbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starbase "SpaceX Starbase").[&#8202;](#ref-back-ars-1)[\[161\]](#refnote-ars) Locals raised concerns about SpaceX's authority, power, and a potential threat for eviction through [eminent domain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eminent_domain "Eminent domain").[&#8202;](#ref-back-165-0)[\[162\]](#refnote-165) In 2025, it was [incorporated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_Starbase_Incorporation_election "City of Starbase Incorporation election") as [Starbase, Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starbase,_Texas "Starbase, Texas").[&#8202;](#ref-back-bloomberg_starbase-0)[\[163\]](#refnote-bloomberg_starbase)

In early April, the orbital launch pad's fuel storage tanks began mounting.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Weber-3)[\[112\]](#refnote-Weber) SN12 through SN14 were scrapped before completion; [SN15](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_SN15 "Starship SN15") was selected to fly instead,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Mack-2021b-0)[\[164\]](#refnote-Mack-2021b) due to improved [avionics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avionics "Avionics"), structure, and engines.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FoustSurvives-1)[\[158\]](#refnote-FoustSurvives) On May 5, 2021, SN15 launched, completed the same maneuvers as older prototypes, and landed safely.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Mack-2021b-1)[\[164\]](#refnote-Mack-2021b) SN15 had a fire in the engine area after landing but it was extinguished.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FoustSurvives-2)[\[158\]](#refnote-FoustSurvives) According to a later report by SpaceX, SN15 experienced several issues while landing, including the loss of tank pressure and an engine.[&#8202;](#ref-back-SpaceX-2023a-0)[\[165\]](#refnote-SpaceX-2023a):2

### Integrated flight tests (2023–)

See also: [List of Starship launches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Starship_launches "List of Starship launches")

In June 2022, the [Federal Aviation Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Aviation_Administration "Federal Aviation Administration") determined that SpaceX must address more than 75 issues identified in the preliminary environmental assessment before flight tests could start.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Chang-2)[\[166\]](#refnote-Chang)

#### First flight test

Main article: [Starship flight test 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_1 "Starship flight test 1")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/15/StarshipLaunch_%28crop_2-3%29.jpg/500px-StarshipLaunch_%28crop_2-3%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:StarshipLaunch_(crop_2-3).jpg) Starship during the first flight attempt. Several engines failed on the first stage.

In July 2022, [Booster 7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Heavy_B7 "Super Heavy B7") tested the liquid oxygen [turbopumps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turbopump "Turbopump") on all 33 Raptor engines, resulting in an explosion at the vehicle's base, which destroyed a pressure pipe and caused minor damage to the launchpad.[&#8202;](#ref-back-170-0)[\[167\]](#refnote-170) By the end of November, Ship 24 had performed 2 static test fires,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Kshatriya-0)[\[168\]](#refnote-Kshatriya):20 while Booster 7 had performed 6 static test fires[&#8202;](#ref-back-172-0)[\[169\]](#refnote-172)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Kshatriya-1)[\[168\]](#refnote-Kshatriya):20 and finally on February 9, 2023, a static fire with 31 engines at 50% throttle.[&#8202;](#ref-back-173-0)[\[170\]](#refnote-173) In January 2023, the whole Starship stack underwent a full [wet dress rehearsal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wet_dress_rehearsal "Wet dress rehearsal").[&#8202;](#ref-back-174-0)[\[171\]](#refnote-174)

After a launch attempt aborted on April 17, 2023,[&#8202;](#ref-back-175-0)[\[172\]](#refnote-175) [Booster 7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Heavy_B7 "Super Heavy B7") and [Ship 24](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_Ship_24 "Starship Ship 24") lifted off on April 20 at 13:33 UTC in the first orbital flight test.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Jackie_Wattles_2023-0)[\[173\]](#refnote-Jackie_Wattles_2023) Three engines were disabled during the launch sequence and several more failed during the flight.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Bergin-0)[\[174\]](#refnote-Bergin) The booster later lost [thrust vectoring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thrust_vectoring "Thrust vectoring") control of the Raptor engines, which led to the rocket spinning out of control.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Bergin-1)[\[174\]](#refnote-Bergin) The vehicle reached a maximum altitude of 24mi (39km).[&#8202;](#ref-back-178-0)[\[175\]](#refnote-178) Approximately 3 minutes after lift-off the rocket's [autonomous flight termination system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Range_safety "Range safety") was activated, though the vehicle tumbled for another 40 seconds before disintegrating.[&#8202;](#ref-back-179-0)[\[176\]](#refnote-179) The first flight test blasted large amounts of sand and soil in the air, reaching communities within a 10.7km (6.6mi) radius.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Leinfelder-0)[\[177\]](#refnote-Leinfelder) A brushfire on nearby state parkland also occurred, burning 3.5 acres (1.4ha) of state parkland.[&#8202;](#ref-back-181-0)[\[178\]](#refnote-181)

#### Second flight test

Main article: [Starship flight test 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_2 "Starship flight test 2")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d7/Starship-IFT2-ascent_%28cropped%29.jpg/500px-Starship-IFT2-ascent_%28cropped%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Starship-IFT2-ascent_(cropped).jpg) Starship during the second flight attempt

After the first test flight, SpaceX began work on the launch mount to repair the damage it sustained during the test and to prevent future issues. The foundation of the launch tower was reinforced and a water-powered [flame deflector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flame_deflector#SpaceX_Starship_launch_mount "Flame deflector") was built under the launch mount.[&#8202;](#ref-back-182-0)[\[179\]](#refnote-182) [Ship 25](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_Ship_25 "Starship Ship 25") and [Booster 9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Heavy_B9 "Super Heavy B9") were rolled to the suborbital and orbital launch sites in May to undergo multiple tests.[&#8202;](#ref-back-183-0)[\[180\]](#refnote-183)

On November 18, 2023, Booster 9 and Ship 25 lifted off the pad.[&#8202;](#ref-back-184-0)[\[181\]](#refnote-184) All 33 engines continued to function until staging, where the second stage separated by pushing itself away from the first stage using a [hot-staging](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hot-staging "Hot-staging") technique.[&#8202;](#ref-back-SpaceX-2023b-1)[\[116\]](#refnote-SpaceX-2023b) Following separation, the Super Heavy booster completed its flip maneuver and initiated the boostback burn, but then experienced multiple successive engine failures and exploded.[&#8202;](#ref-back-185-0)[\[182\]](#refnote-185) Blockage in a liquid oxygen filter caused one of the engines to fail in a way that resulted in the destruction of the booster, which occurred three and a half minutes into the flight at an altitude of ~90km over the Gulf of Mexico.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ars-ift2-postmortem-0)[\[183\]](#refnote-ars-ift2-postmortem)

The second stage continued until it reached an altitude of ~149 kilometres (93mi), after over eight minutes of flight; before engine cutoff, telemetry was lost on the second stage.[&#8202;](#ref-back-SpaceX-2023b-2)[\[116\]](#refnote-SpaceX-2023b) SpaceX said that a safe command based on flight performance data triggered the flight termination system and destroyed the second stage,[&#8202;](#ref-back-SpaceX-2023b-3)[\[116\]](#refnote-SpaceX-2023b) before completing its planned suborbital trajectory or attempting re-entry.[&#8202;](#ref-back-187-0)[\[184\]](#refnote-187) It appeared to re-enter a few hundred miles north of the [Virgin Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virgin_Islands "Virgin Islands"), according to [NOAA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Oceanic_and_Atmospheric_Administration "National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration") weather radar data.[&#8202;](#ref-back-188-0)[\[185\]](#refnote-188)

#### Third flight test

Main article: [Starship flight test 3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_3 "Starship flight test 3")

Video of Starship during the third flight test

Following the second flight test (which saw the loss of both stages), significant changes were implemented, including upgrading Starship's thrust vector control system to electric [thrust vector control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thrust_vectoring "Thrust vectoring") (TVC)[&#8202;](#ref-back-SpaceX-2024b-0)[\[186\]](#refnote-SpaceX-2024b) and measures to delay [liquid oxygen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liquid_oxygen "Liquid oxygen") (LOX)[&#8202;](#ref-back-SpaceX-2024b-1)[\[186\]](#refnote-SpaceX-2024b) venting until after Starship engine cutoff (SECO) has taken place.

Flight 3 launched from the [SpaceX Starbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starbase "SpaceX Starbase") facility along the [South Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Texas "South Texas") coast around 8:25a.m. CDT on March 14, 2024, coincidentally the 22nd anniversary of the founding of SpaceX.[&#8202;](#ref-back-190-0)[\[187\]](#refnote-190) Like flight 2, all 33 engines on the booster ignited and stage separation was successful.[&#8202;](#ref-back-nsf-stream-0)[\[188\]](#refnote-nsf-stream) B10 conducted a [boostback burn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boostback_burn "Boostback burn"), however, the planned [landing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_landing "Water landing") in the [Gulf of Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_Mexico "Gulf of Mexico") was not successful, as it exploded at 462m (1,516ft) above the surface.[&#8202;](#ref-back-SpaceX-2024a-1)[\[118\]](#refnote-SpaceX-2024a)

The Starship spacecraft itself, after reaching space and orbital velocity, conducted several tests after engine cutoff, including initiating a [propellant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Propellant "Propellant") transfer demo and payload dispenser test.[&#8202;](#ref-back-192-0)[\[189\]](#refnote-192) It attempted to [re-enter the atmosphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atmospheric_entry "Atmospheric entry"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-Berger-0)[\[190\]](#refnote-Berger) and at an altitude of around 65km (40mi), all telemetry from Ship 28 stopped, indicating a loss of the vehicle.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ars-ift3-progress-0)[\[191\]](#refnote-ars-ift3-progress) This flight test demonstrated a cryogenic propellant transfer, by transferring propellant from the Ship's header tanks into its main tanks while in space, a technology which is required for Starship HLS to exit [Low Earth orbit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Low_Earth_orbit "Low Earth orbit") (LEO). The result of this test was declared successful by NASA and SpaceX. Additional data analysis is occurring on the [fluid dynamics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fluid_dynamics "Fluid dynamics") such as slosh and boil-off of the propellant.[&#8202;](#ref-back-abc-0)[\[192\]](#refnote-abc)

#### Fourth flight test

Main article: [Starship flight test 4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_4 "Starship flight test 4")

The fourth flight test of the full Starship configuration launched on June 6, 2024, at 7:50a.m. CDT.[&#8202;](#ref-back-196-0)[\[193\]](#refnote-196) The goals for the test flight were for the Super Heavy booster to land on a 'virtual tower' in the ocean, and for the Ship to survive peak heating during atmospheric reentry.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Davenport-0)[\[194\]](#refnote-Davenport) The flight test was successful in both regards, with Super Heavy achieving a soft splashdown and Ship surviving atmospheric reentry and a controlled splashdown despite significant damage to the flight control surfaces.[&#8202;](#ref-back-198-0)[\[195\]](#refnote-198)

#### Fifth flight test

Main article: [Starship flight test 5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_5 "Starship flight test 5")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c2/Starship_Booster_Landing_on_Mechzilla_%2854064036815%29.jpg/500px-Starship_Booster_Landing_on_Mechzilla_%2854064036815%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Starship_Booster_Landing_on_Mechzilla_(54064036815).jpg) [Booster 12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Heavy_Booster_12 "Super Heavy Booster 12") is successfully caught by the launch tower during flight test 5.

In April 2024, Musk stated one of the goals was to attempt a booster tower landing based on successful booster performance in flight 4. Vehicle testing commenced in May 2024.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Flight_4-0)[\[196\]](#refnote-Flight_4) SpaceX claimed that B12 and S30 were ready to launch in early August, in advance of regulatory approval.[&#8202;](#ref-back-200-0)[\[197\]](#refnote-200) SpaceX flew S30 and B12 on October 13, 2024, with B12 returning to the launch site for a successful catch for the first time, and S30 successfully splashing down in the Indian Ocean.[&#8202;](#ref-back-201-0)[\[198\]](#refnote-201)

#### Sixth flight test

Main article: [Starship flight test 6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_6 "Starship flight test 6")

Ship 31 completed a successful cryogenic test in July 2024 and a static fire in September.[&#8202;](#ref-back-202-0)[\[199\]](#refnote-202) Flight 6 was flown on November 19, 2024, with a water landing of the booster rather than a catch.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NASASpaceflight-2024c-0)[\[200\]](#refnote-NASASpaceflight-2024c) Flight 6 was the first to successfully conduct a Raptor engine relight in the vacuum of space, paving the way for [payload](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Payload "Payload") deployments on future flights.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NASASpaceflight-2024c-1)[\[200\]](#refnote-NASASpaceflight-2024c) A [stuffed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stuffed_toy "Stuffed toy") toy [banana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banana "Banana") served as the zero-g indicator, becoming Starship's first payload, though it remained within the vehicle for the duration of the flight.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NASASpaceflight-2024c-2)[\[200\]](#refnote-NASASpaceflight-2024c) [Eric Berger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eric_Berger_(journalist) "Eric Berger (journalist)") claimed that due to the success of the in-space relight, Starship would likely be "cleared to travel into orbit".[&#8202;](#ref-back-204-0)[\[201\]](#refnote-204)

#### Seventh flight test

Main article: [Starship flight test 7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_7 "Starship flight test 7")

Ship 33 completed a successful cryogenic test in October 2024[&#8202;](#ref-back-205-0)[\[202\]](#refnote-205) and a static fire in late December.[&#8202;](#ref-back-206-0)[\[203\]](#refnote-206) Booster 14 rolled out to OLP-1 and conducted a successful spin prime test and static fire in early December.[&#8202;](#ref-back-207-0)[\[204\]](#refnote-207) Flight 7 was flown on January 16, 2025; the mission profile for flight test 7 was expected to be similar to the previous launch, targeting a splashdown in the Indian Ocean after attempting an in-space engine relight. Ship 33 was also expected to deploy ten [Starlink](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starlink "Starlink") "simulators," which were also expected to reenter over the Indian Ocean.[&#8202;](#ref-back-208-0)[\[205\]](#refnote-208) Contact with Ship 33 was lost shortly before its engines were scheduled to shut down.[&#8202;](#ref-back-SS-0)[\[206\]](#refnote-SS) Subsequently, Ship 33 was seen exploding as it flew over the [Turks and Caicos Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turks_and_Caicos_Islands "Turks and Caicos Islands"). The booster successfully returned to the launch site and was caught by the chopsticks on OLP-A.[&#8202;](#ref-back-SS-1)[\[206\]](#refnote-SS) As a result of the explosion, numerous commercial airline flights were diverted or delayed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-210-0)[\[207\]](#refnote-210)

#### Eighth flight test

Main article: [Starship flight test 8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_8 "Starship flight test 8")

On March 3, 2025, a launch attempt was aborted after multiple holds at _T_ −40 seconds. Shortly before the scheduled launch at 6:45p.m. CDT, an issue caused a hold for more than five minutes. The hold was briefly lifted, but a new hold was put in place due to issues connected to the Super Heavy booster.[&#8202;](#ref-back-211-0)[\[208\]](#refnote-211) SpaceX called for a cancellation of the launch and set March 6 for the launch.[&#8202;](#ref-back-212-0)[\[209\]](#refnote-212) The eighth flight test was later launched on March 6, 2025, at 23:30 hours UTC. The Super Heavy booster was successfully caught by the launch tower. During Ship 34's initial burn, four of its six engines experienced premature shutdowns that resulted in a loss of attitude control followed by a total loss of telemetry. The vehicle's breakup was observed from [Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida "Florida"), [Jamaica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jamaica "Jamaica"), and the [Turks and Caicos Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turks_and_Caicos_Islands "Turks and Caicos Islands").[&#8202;](#ref-back-213-0)[\[210\]](#refnote-213) According to SpaceX, communications with the spacecraft ended 9minutes and 30seconds after liftoff.[&#8202;](#ref-back-214-0)[\[211\]](#refnote-214) The flight was the second of the Block 2 Ship, and attempted to repeat the previous flight's profile.[&#8202;](#ref-back-SpaceX_Flight_8-0)[\[212\]](#refnote-SpaceX_Flight_8) Due to the breakup of the vehicles, the Federal Aviation Administration briefly issued ground stop orders for multiple Florida airports.[&#8202;](#ref-back-216-0)[\[213\]](#refnote-216)

#### Ninth flight test

Main article: [Starship flight test 9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_9 "Starship flight test 9")

Starship's [ninth flight test](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_9 "Starship flight test 9") launched on May 27, 2025, with Booster 14, the program's first reused Super Heavy booster.[&#8202;](#ref-back-SL-0)[\[214\]](#refnote-SL) The booster completed its ascent, executed a boost-back and entry burn and re-entered at a higher angle of attack than previous flights, but was lost before its planned splashdown in the Gulf of Mexico.[&#8202;](#ref-back-218-0)[\[215\]](#refnote-218) Ship 35 reached engine cutoff, yet a propellant leak caused loss of attitude control preventing reignition of a raptor engine and the payload bay door failed to open preventing deployment of the dummy starlink satellites; the vehicle broke up during re-entry.[&#8202;](#ref-back-SL-1)[\[214\]](#refnote-SL)

#### Ship 36 explosion

On June 18, 2025, Ship 36, the Block 2 upper stage slated for the program's tenth flight test, exploded during a planned six-engine static-fire at SpaceX's Massey test stand near Starbase, Texas.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NSFShip36-0)[\[216\]](#refnote-NSFShip36) Preliminary analysis suggested the failure of a composite-overwrapped pressure vessel (COPV) in the nose section, which ruptured and ignited methane and liquid-oxygen propellants.[&#8202;](#ref-back-SFN2025-0)[\[217\]](#refnote-SFN2025) No personnel were injured, but SpaceX paused further testing to inspect Block 2 pressurization hardware and repair the damaged stand.[&#8202;](#ref-back-221-0)[\[218\]](#refnote-221)

#### Tenth flight test

Main article: [Starship flight test 10](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_10 "Starship flight test 10")

Starship's [tenth flight test](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_10 "Starship flight test 10") was scheduled for August 24, 2025, but was scrubbed shortly before liftoff. After a second aborted attempt on August 25, Flight 10 launched on August 26.[&#8202;](#ref-back-SFN-F10-0)[\[219\]](#refnote-SFN-F10) The booster completed its ascent and boostback, then performed a landing burn experiment, intentionally disabling one center engine and using a backup engine, before hovering and splashing down in the Gulf of Mexico. The ship deployed eight Starlink simulators, the first successful payload demonstration from Starship, and relit a Raptor engine in space. It then reentered and made a controlled splashdown on target in the Indian Ocean.[&#8202;](#ref-back-SpaceX-F10-0)[\[220\]](#refnote-SpaceX-F10)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d9/Starship_lifted_off_from_Starbase_during_flight_test_11.jpg/500px-Starship_lifted_off_from_Starbase_during_flight_test_11.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Starship_lifted_off_from_Starbase_during_flight_test_11.jpg) Liftoff Of Starship flight test eleven

#### Eleventh flight test

Main article: [Starship flight test 11](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_11 "Starship flight test 11")

Starship's [eleventh flight test](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_11 "Starship flight test 11") launched on October 13, 2025, largely repeating the mission profile of the tenth flight.[&#8202;](#ref-back-SN-F11-0)[\[221\]](#refnote-SN-F11) The reused Booster 15-2 completed its boostback burn with 12 of 13 planned engines. The engine that had failed to relight came back for the landing burn, which tested an engine configuration planned for the next-generation booster. The booster then hovered and splashed down in the Gulf of Mexico.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NSF-F11-0)[\[222\]](#refnote-NSF-F11) Ship 38 deployed eight Starlink simulators and performed the third in-space Raptor relight. During reentry it flew a dynamic banking maneuver to mimic the trajectory of future returns to Starbase, then made a landing flip, landing burn and soft splashdown in the Indian Ocean.[&#8202;](#ref-back-SpaceX-F11-0)[\[223\]](#refnote-SpaceX-F11) This was the final flight of the Block 2 Starship and of the original Pad 1 configuration.[&#8202;](#ref-back-SpaceX-F11-1)[\[223\]](#refnote-SpaceX-F11)

#### Twelfth flight test

Main article: [Starship flight test 12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_12 "Starship flight test 12")

Starship's twelfth flight test launched on May 22, 2026, using Booster 19 and Ship 39. It was the first flight of the Starship V3 (Block 3) vehicles and Raptor 3 engines, and the first launch from Starbase's Pad 2.[&#8202;](#ref-back-SpaceX-F12-1)[\[110\]](#refnote-SpaceX-F12) One of the booster's 33 engines shut down during ascent. After hot staging, the booster could not light all the engines planned for its boostback burn, ended the burn early, and hit the Gulf of Mexico hard after a landing-burn attempt.[&#8202;](#ref-back-SpaceX-F12-2)[\[110\]](#refnote-SpaceX-F12)[&#8202;](#ref-back-SFN-F12-0)[\[224\]](#refnote-SFN-F12) Ship 39 lost one of its three Raptor Vacuum engines during ascent but still reached its planned trajectory. It deployed 22 mock satellites, two of which carried cameras to image the ship's heat shield. SpaceX skipped the planned in-space engine relight, and the ship made a controlled splashdown in the Indian Ocean.[&#8202;](#ref-back-CNN-F12-0)[\[225\]](#refnote-CNN-F12)[&#8202;](#ref-back-SFN-F12-1)[\[224\]](#refnote-SFN-F12) The FAA classified the booster's loss as a mishap and required a SpaceX-led investigation before Flight 13.[&#8202;](#ref-back-SFN-F12-2)[\[224\]](#refnote-SFN-F12)

#### Thirteenth flight test

Main article: [Starship flight test 13](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_13 "Starship flight test 13")

Aiming to complete the same goals as flight 12, except with functional Starlink V3 satellites instead of [mass simulators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_simulator "Mass simulator"), Starship's [thirteenth flight test](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_13 "Starship flight test 13") was initially scheduled for July 16, 2026, but the attempt was aborted at the last second because of engine issues, and SpaceX replaced some first-stage Raptor engines.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Space-F13-0)[\[226\]](#refnote-Space-F13) After a second scrub on July 23 due to weather, Flight 13 successfully launched on July 24 with booster 20 and ship 40. Booster 20 completed its ascent, stage separation and boostback, then attempted a soft splashdown in the Gulf of Mexico. Only five of the 13 engines planned for the landing burn appeared to ignite, and the booster hit the water harder than planned.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Space-F13-1)[\[226\]](#refnote-Space-F13) Ship 40 nominally completed suborbital insertion and deployed 20 Starlink V3 satellites before successfully conducting an in-vacuum relight of one sea-level Raptor engine. Ship continued through reentry and achieved soft splashdown in the Indian Ocean, becoming the first Starship to remain intact after toppling over post-splashdown. SpaceX later recovered the floating ship and returned it to Starbase.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Space-F13-2)[\[226\]](#refnote-Space-F13)[&#8202;](#ref-back-SPOOrbit-0)[\[227\]](#refnote-SPOOrbit)[&#8202;](#ref-back-NSF-F15-0)[\[228\]](#refnote-NSF-F15)

#### Fourteenth flight test

Main article: [Starship flight test 14](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_14 "Starship flight test 14")

Marking a significant expansion of Starship's objectives, on September 28, 2026, flight 14 entered [Earth orbit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earth_orbit "Earth orbit") and completed the first operational [Starlink](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starlink "Starlink") deployment.[&#8202;](#ref-back-232-0)[\[229\]](#refnote-232) The mission deployed 26 operational Starlink V3 satellites.[&#8202;](#ref-back-SPOOrbit-1)[\[227\]](#refnote-SPOOrbit) During Ship 41's ascent, one of its three [Raptor Vacuum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raptor_Vacuum "Raptor Vacuum") engines shut down prematurely.[&#8202;](#ref-back-SPOOrbit-2)[\[227\]](#refnote-SPOOrbit) SpaceX initially announced that the spacecraft would not commit to orbit, but reversed that decision after evaluating its condition.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ReutersOrbit2-0)[\[230\]](#refnote-ReutersOrbit2) Approximately 25 minutes after liftoff, Ship 41 performed a short orbital-insertion burn using a single Raptor engine and entered orbit.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ReutersOrbit2-1)[\[230\]](#refnote-ReutersOrbit2)[&#8202;](#ref-back-SPOOrbit-3)[\[227\]](#refnote-SPOOrbit) SpaceX ended the flight early instead of completing the planned six-orbit, nearly 10-hour mission that was to end in the South Pacific west of Chile. Ship 41 splashed down in the North Pacific about 3 hours and 8 minutes after liftoff, then tipped over and exploded.[&#8202;](#ref-back-SPOOrbit-4)[\[227\]](#refnote-SPOOrbit) The booster lost one of its 33 engines shortly after liftoff and made a soft splashdown in the Gulf of Mexico. No tower catch was attempted.[&#8202;](#ref-back-SPOOrbit-5)[\[227\]](#refnote-SPOOrbit)[&#8202;](#ref-back-APOrbit-0)[\[231\]](#refnote-APOrbit)[&#8202;](#ref-back-ReutersOrbit2-2)[\[230\]](#refnote-ReutersOrbit2)

#### Fifteenth flight test

Main article: [Starship flight 15](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_15 "Starship flight 15")

As of October 2026, Flight 15 is expected no earlier than November 2026, with Booster 22 and Ship 42. SpaceX plans to try to catch both the Super Heavy booster and, for the first time, the Starship upper stage using two launch towers.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NSF-F15-1)[\[228\]](#refnote-NSF-F15)[&#8202;](#ref-back-235-0)[\[232\]](#refnote-235)

## Cost and funding

SpaceX develops the Starship primarily with [private funding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_capital "Private capital").[&#8202;](#ref-back-ars20190929-1)[\[136\]](#refnote-ars20190929) SpaceX Chief Financial Officer [Bret Johnsen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bret_Johnsen "Bret Johnsen") disclosed in court that SpaceX has invested more than $3 billion into the [Starbase facility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starbase "SpaceX Starbase") and Starship systems from July 2014 to May 2023.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Kolodny-0)[\[233\]](#refnote-Kolodny) Elon Musk stated in April 2023 that SpaceX expected to spend about $2 billion on Starship development in 2023.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Maidenberg-0)[\[234\]](#refnote-Maidenberg) In an October 2024 court filing opposing an environmental group's request to halt launches, SpaceX stated that maintaining Starbase and the Starship development program cost approximately $4 million per day. It added that each day of delay to milestone revenue would cost over $100,000 per day for every billion dollars delayed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-SaveRGV-filing-0)[\[235\]](#refnote-SaveRGV-filing):26–27

Musk has theorized that a Starship orbital launch might eventually cost SpaceX only $1 million to launch.[&#8202;](#ref-back-239-0)[\[236\]](#refnote-239) [Eurospace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurospace "Eurospace")'s director of research Pierre Lionnet stated in 2022 that Starship's launch price to customers would likely be higher because of the rocket's development cost.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Scoles-0)[\[237\]](#refnote-Scoles) In an update on their website in late October 2025,[&#8202;](#ref-back-241-0)[\[238\]](#refnote-241) SpaceX stated regarding Starship that "SpaceX is self-funding representing over 90% of system costs"\[ _[sic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sic "Sic")_\].[&#8202;](#ref-back-242-0)[\[239\]](#refnote-242)

As part of the development of the [Human Landing System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Landing_System "Human Landing System") for the [Artemis program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artemis_program "Artemis program"), SpaceX was awarded in April 2021 a $2.89 billion fixed-price contract from NASA to develop the [Starship lunar lander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_HLS "Starship HLS") for [Artemis III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artemis_III "Artemis III").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Brown-0)[\[240\]](#refnote-Brown) [Blue Origin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Origin "Blue Origin"), a bidding competitor to SpaceX, disputed the decision and [began a legal case against NASA and SpaceX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Origin_v._United_States_&_Space_Exploration_Technologies_Corp. "Blue Origin v. United States & Space Exploration Technologies Corp.") in August 2021, causing NASA to suspend the contract for three months until the case was dismissed in the [Court of Federal Claims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Court_of_Federal_Claims "United States Court of Federal Claims").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Pruitt-Young-0)[\[241\]](#refnote-Pruitt-Young) Two years later Blue Origin was awarded a $3.4 billion fixed-price contract for [its lunar lander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Moon_(spacecraft) "Blue Moon (spacecraft)").[&#8202;](#ref-back-O'Shea-0)[\[242\]](#refnote-O'Shea)

In 2022, NASA awarded SpaceX a $1.15 billion fixed-price contract for a second lunar lander for [Artemis IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artemis_IV "Artemis IV").[&#8202;](#ref-back-news.yahoo.com-0)[\[243\]](#refnote-news.yahoo.com) The same year, SpaceX was awarded a $102million five-year contract to develop the [Rocket Cargo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rocket_Cargo "Rocket Cargo") program for the [United States Space Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Space_Force "United States Space Force").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Erwin-0)[\[244\]](#refnote-Erwin)

### 2023

| Flight<br>No. | Date and<br>time ( [UTC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coordinated_Universal_Time "Coordinated Universal Time")) | Version,<br>booster[&#8202;](#ref-back-vehicle-0)[\[d\]](#refnote-vehicle) | Version,<br>ship[&#8202;](#ref-back-vehicle-1)[\[d\]](#refnote-vehicle) | Launch site | Payload | Payload mass | Orbit | Customer | Launch outcome | Booster landing | Ship landing |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | April 20, 2023<br>13:33:09[&#8202;](#ref-back-249-0)[\[245\]](#refnote-249) | [Block 1](#Block_1)<br>[B7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Heavy_B7 "Super Heavy B7") | [Block 1](#Block_1)<br>[S24](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_Ship_24 "Starship Ship 24") | [Starbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starbase "SpaceX Starbase"), [OLP‑1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_Launch_Pad_1 "Orbital Launch Pad 1") | —N/a | —N/a | [Transatmospheric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transatmospheric_orbit "Transatmospheric orbit")[&#8202;](#ref-back-jsp819-0)[\[246\]](#refnote-jsp819) | [SpaceX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX "SpaceX") | Failure | Precluded | Precluded |
| [Flight 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_1 "Starship flight test 1") was the first with a ship integrated with the [Super Heavy booster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Super_Heavy "SpaceX Super Heavy"), the booster was planned to make a powered splashdown in the Gulf of Mexico, and the ship would enter a [transatmospheric Earth orbit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transatmospheric_orbit "Transatmospheric orbit") before reentering and impacting the Pacific Ocean north of Hawaii. Several engines were shut down before and during flight. The vehicle eventually entered an uncontrolled spin before stage separation due to loss of [thrust vector control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thrust_vector_control "Thrust vector control"). The [flight termination system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flight_termination_system "Flight termination system") activated but the vehicle remained intact for 40 more seconds.[&#8202;](#ref-back-251-0)[\[247\]](#refnote-251) SpaceX declared this flight a success, as their primary goal was to only clear the pad.[&#8202;](#ref-back-252-0)[\[248\]](#refnote-252) The launch resulted in extensive damage to the orbital launch mount and the infrastructures around it, including the propellant tank farm.[&#8202;](#ref-back-253-0)[\[249\]](#refnote-253) |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| 2 | November 18, 2023<br>13:02:50[&#8202;](#ref-back-254-0)[\[250\]](#refnote-254) | [Block 1](#Block_1)<br>[B9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Heavy_B9 "Super Heavy B9") | [Block 1](#Block_1)<br>[S25](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_Ship_25 "Starship Ship 25") | [Starbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starbase "SpaceX Starbase"), [OLP‑1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_Launch_Pad_1 "Orbital Launch Pad 1") | —N/a | —N/a | [Transatmospheric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transatmospheric_orbit "Transatmospheric orbit")[&#8202;](#ref-back-jsp826-0)[\[251\]](#refnote-jsp826) | [SpaceX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX "SpaceX") | Failure | Failure (gulf) | Precluded |
| [Flight 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_2 "Starship flight test 2") had a test flight profile similar to the first flight, with the addition of a new hot-staging technique and the introduction of a water deluge system as part of the ground support equipment at the launch pad. During the first stage ascent, all 33 engines fired to full duration. Starship and Super Heavy successfully accomplished a [hot-staging separation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hot_staging "Hot staging"). After initiating a flip maneuver and initiating boostback burn, the booster suffered engine shutdowns and was lost.[&#8202;](#ref-back-SpaceX_2024-0)[\[252\]](#refnote-SpaceX_2024-02) The upper stage ascended nominally[&#8202;](#ref-back-257-0)[\[253\]](#refnote-257) until the automated flight termination system destroyed it due to loss of communications from a fire.[&#8202;](#ref-back-SpaceX_2024-1)[\[252\]](#refnote-SpaceX_2024-02) |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |

### 2024

| Flight<br>No. | Date and<br>time ( [UTC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coordinated_Universal_Time "Coordinated Universal Time")) | Version,<br>booster[&#8202;](#ref-back-vehicle-2)[\[d\]](#refnote-vehicle) | Version,<br>ship[&#8202;](#ref-back-vehicle-3)[\[d\]](#refnote-vehicle) | Launch site | Payload | Payload mass | Orbit | Customer | Launch outcome | Booster landing | Ship landing |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 3 | March 14, 2024<br>13:25:00[&#8202;](#ref-back-258-0)[\[254\]](#refnote-258) | [Block 1](#Block_1)<br>[B10](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Heavy_Booster_10 "Super Heavy Booster 10") | [Block 1](#Block_1)<br>[S28](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_Ship_28 "Starship Ship 28") | [Starbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starbase "SpaceX Starbase"), [OLP‑1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_Launch_Pad_1 "Orbital Launch Pad 1") | —N/a | —N/a | [Suborbital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sub-orbital_spaceflight "Sub-orbital spaceflight")[&#8202;](#ref-back-JSR-0)[\[255\]](#refnote-JSR) | [SpaceX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX "SpaceX") | Success | Failure (gulf) | Failure (ocean) |
| [Flight 3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_3 "Starship flight test 3") included a full-duration burn of the second-stage engines, an internal propellant-transfer demonstration, and a test of the Starlink dispenser door. The planned in-space engine relight of the spacecraft and its hard splashdown into the Indian Ocean did not occur.[&#8202;](#ref-back-260-0)[\[256\]](#refnote-260) The booster successfully propelled the spacecraft to staging, with 13 engines ignited for a boostback burn, though six engines shut down prematurely. During the landing burn ignition, only three engines ignited, and the booster was lost around 400 meters above the ocean.[&#8202;](#ref-back-SpaceX_3rd_test-0)[\[257\]](#refnote-SpaceX_3rd_test) At reentry, Ship had an uncontrolled roll and lost telemetry, leading SpaceX to conclude the ship was lost during reentry. |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| 4 | June 6, 2024<br>12:50:00[&#8202;](#ref-back-262-0)[\[258\]](#refnote-262) | [Block 1](#Block_1)<br>[B11](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Heavy_Booster_11 "Super Heavy Booster 11") | [Block 1](#Block_1)<br>[S29](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_Ship_29 "Starship Ship 29") | [Starbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starbase "SpaceX Starbase"), [OLP‑1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_Launch_Pad_1 "Orbital Launch Pad 1") | —N/a | —N/a | [Suborbital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sub-orbital_spaceflight "Sub-orbital spaceflight")[&#8202;](#ref-back-jsr834-0)[\[259\]](#refnote-jsr834) | [SpaceX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX "SpaceX") | Success | Controlled (gulf) | Controlled (ocean) |
| [Flight 4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_4 "Starship flight test 4") flew a similar trajectory to Flight 3, with the addition of a ship landing burn and soft splashdown. One Raptor engine was lost shortly after liftoff, but the booster performed in accordance to its flight profile and conducted a controlled splashdown in the Gulf of Mexico[&#8202;](#ref-back-264-0)[\[260\]](#refnote-264) on a "virtual tower", in preparation for a catch by the launch tower during Flight 5.[&#8202;](#ref-back-265-0)[\[261\]](#refnote-265) The spacecraft performed a successful reentry despite severe forward flap damage and conducted a controlled splashdown in the Indian Ocean,[&#8202;](#ref-back-266-0)[\[262\]](#refnote-266) within the target region but 6 kilometers from the center.[&#8202;](#ref-back-267-0)[\[263\]](#refnote-267) |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| 5 | October 13, 2024<br>12:25:00[&#8202;](#ref-back-268-0)[\[264\]](#refnote-268) | [Block 1](#Block_1)<br>[B12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Heavy_Booster_12 "Super Heavy Booster 12") | [Block 1](#Block_1)<br>[S30](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_Ship_30 "Starship Ship 30") | [Starbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starbase "SpaceX Starbase"), [OLP‑1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_Launch_Pad_1 "Orbital Launch Pad 1") | —N/a | —N/a | [Suborbital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sub-orbital_spaceflight "Sub-orbital spaceflight")[&#8202;](#ref-back-sn20241009-0)[\[265\]](#refnote-sn20241009) | [SpaceX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX "SpaceX") | Success | Success (OLP-1) | Controlled (ocean) |
| [Flight 5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_5 "Starship flight test 5") was the first to achieve booster recovery and complete a flight without engine failures. After stage separation, the booster [returned to the launch site](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Launch_vehicle#Return_to_launch_site "Launch vehicle") and was caught by the launch tower arms despite damage to a chine during descent. Following a coast phase, Ship 30 reentered the atmosphere, performed reentry despite forward flap damage, and executed a landing burn, splashing down at its target in the Indian Ocean.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Weber_2024-0)[\[266\]](#refnote-Weber_2024) |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| 6 | November 19, 2024<br>22:00:00[&#8202;](#ref-back-space20241119-0)[\[267\]](#refnote-space20241119) | [Block 1](#Block_1)<br>[B13](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Heavy_Booster_13 "Super Heavy Booster 13") | [Block 1](#Block_1)<br>[S31](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_Ship_31 "Starship Ship 31") | [Starbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starbase "SpaceX Starbase"), [OLP‑1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_Launch_Pad_1 "Orbital Launch Pad 1") | [Plush banana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stuffed_toy "Stuffed toy") | Unknown | [Transatmospheric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transatmospheric_orbit "Transatmospheric orbit")[&#8202;](#ref-back-272-0)[\[268\]](#refnote-272) | [SpaceX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX "SpaceX") | Success | Controlled (gulf)<br>Abort (OLP‑1) | Controlled (ocean) |
| [Flight 6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_6 "Starship flight test 6") was the second attempt at booster recovery and the final use of a Block 1 upper stage. Heat shield tiles were removed from key areas of Ship 31, which also lacked the ablative backup layer from Flight 5. Following stage separation, the booster was diverted to the ocean near the launch site due to damage to the catch tower during liftoff. The ship completed an in-space engine relight test and re-entered, splashing down in the Indian Ocean during daylight—a first for Starship. Despite a reduced heat shield and steeper re-entry trajectory, Ship 31 sustained minimal flap damage. The flight also carried Starship's first payload, a toy stuffed banana serving as the zero-gravity indicator, which remained onboard throughout the mission.[&#8202;](#ref-back-nsf20241118-0)[\[269\]](#refnote-nsf20241118) |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |

### 2025

In a talk in November 2024, Starbase General Manager [Kathy Lueders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kathy_Lueders "Kathy Lueders") announced that SpaceX hoped to be able to catch a [Starship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starship_(spacecraft) "SpaceX Starship (spacecraft)") upper stage sometime in the next 6 months and have as many as 25 launches in 2025.[&#8202;](#ref-back-274-0)[\[270\]](#refnote-274) This did not occur. Starship was launched five times in 2025.

| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 7 | January 16, 2025<br>22:37:00[&#8202;](#ref-back-275-0)[\[271\]](#refnote-275) | [Block 2](#Block_2)<br>[B14‑1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Heavy_Booster_14 "Super Heavy Booster 14")[&#8202;](#ref-back-276-0)[\[272\]](#refnote-276) | [Block 2](#Block_2)<br>[S33](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_Ship_33 "Starship Ship 33")[&#8202;](#ref-back-McCrea_2024-0)[\[273\]](#refnote-McCrea_2024-07) | [Starbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starbase "SpaceX Starbase"), [OLP‑1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_Launch_Pad_1 "Orbital Launch Pad 1") | 10 [Starlink](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starlink "Starlink") simulator satellites[&#8202;](#ref-back-278-0)[\[274\]](#refnote-278) | ~20,000kg (44,000lb)[&#8202;](#ref-back-279-0)[\[275\]](#refnote-279)[&#8202;](#ref-back-280-0)[\[276\]](#refnote-280) | [Transatmospheric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transatmospheric_orbit "Transatmospheric orbit")[&#8202;](#ref-back-jsp841-0)[\[277\]](#refnote-jsp841) | [SpaceX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX "SpaceX") | Failure | Success (OLP-1) | Precluded |
| [Flight 7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_7 "Starship flight test 7") was to follow a trajectory similar to the previous mission, with a planned splashdown in the Indian Ocean approximately one hour post-launch.[&#8202;](#ref-back-McCrea_2024-1)[\[278\]](#refnote-McCrea_2024-11) It marked the inaugural flight of a Block 2 Ship, featuring structural, avionics, and other upgrades.[&#8202;](#ref-back-McCrea_2024-2)[\[273\]](#refnote-McCrea_2024-07) The mission also aimed to test the deployment system for 10 [Starlink](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starlink "Starlink") mass simulator satellites. During the Ship's initial burn, its engines experienced premature shutdowns due to a propellant leak larger than the Ship's systems could handle, followed by a total loss of telemetry. SpaceX stated the autonomous flight safety system destroyed the Ship about three minutes after loss of telemetry.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NY-0)[\[279\]](#refnote-NY) The booster successfully returned to the launch site, where it was caught by the launch tower arms on OLP-1 without noticeable damage to the chines.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Clark_2025-0)[\[280\]](#refnote-Clark_2025) |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| 8 | March 6, 2025<br>23:31:02[&#8202;](#ref-back-SF8-0)[\[281\]](#refnote-SF8) | [Block 2](#Block_2)<br>[B15‑1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Heavy_Booster_15 "Super Heavy Booster 15")[&#8202;](#ref-back-McCrea_2024-3)[\[278\]](#refnote-McCrea_2024-11) | [Block 2](#Block_2)<br>[S34](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_Ship_34 "Starship Ship 34")[&#8202;](#ref-back-Bergin_2024-0)[\[282\]](#refnote-Bergin_2024) | [Starbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starbase "SpaceX Starbase"), [OLP‑1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_Launch_Pad_1 "Orbital Launch Pad 1") | 4 [Starlink](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starlink "Starlink") simulator satellites[&#8202;](#ref-back-SF8-1)[\[281\]](#refnote-SF8) | ~8,000kg (18,000lb)[&#8202;](#ref-back-SF8-2)[\[281\]](#refnote-SF8) | [Transatmospheric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transatmospheric_orbit "Transatmospheric orbit") | [SpaceX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX "SpaceX") | Failure | Success (OLP-1) | Precluded |
| [Flight 8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_8 "Starship flight test 8") was to follow a trajectory similar to the previous mission, with a planned splashdown in the Indian Ocean. During the Ship's initial burn, several engines shut down and the Ship lost control and later telemetry.[&#8202;](#ref-back-287-0)[\[283\]](#refnote-287) The booster was successfully commanded to return to the launch site despite having two engines fail to relight for its boostback burn. One of the failed engines managed to reignite for the catch, which was successful.[&#8202;](#ref-back-SF8-3)[\[281\]](#refnote-SF8) |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| 9 | May 27, 2025<br>23:36:28[&#8202;](#ref-back-spacexflight9-0)[\[284\]](#refnote-spacexflight9) | [Block 2](#Block_2)<br>[B14-2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Heavy_Booster_14 "Super Heavy Booster 14")[&#8202;](#ref-back-289-0)[\[285\]](#refnote-289) | [Block 2](#Block_2)<br>[S35](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_Ship_35 "Starship Ship 35")[&#8202;](#ref-back-290-0)[\[286\]](#refnote-290) | [Starbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starbase "SpaceX Starbase"), [OLP‑1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_Launch_Pad_1 "Orbital Launch Pad 1") | 8 [Starlink](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starlink "Starlink") simulator satellites[&#8202;](#ref-back-spacexflight9-1)[\[284\]](#refnote-spacexflight9) | ~16,000kg (35,000lb)[&#8202;](#ref-back-spacexflight9-2)[\[284\]](#refnote-spacexflight9) | [Transatmospheric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transatmospheric_orbit "Transatmospheric orbit") | [SpaceX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX "SpaceX") | Failure | Failure (gulf) | Failure (ocean) |
| [Flight 9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_9 "Starship flight test 9") was the first to reuse a Super Heavy booster, along with 29 engines having been used on a flight, which completed ascent and boostback into a high angle of attack but was lost before splashdown in the Gulf of Mexico.[&#8202;](#ref-back-F9-0)[\[287\]](#refnote-F9) The ship reached engine cutoff but failed to deploy its payload of eight [Starlink](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starlink "Starlink") simulator satellites[&#8202;](#ref-back-spacexflight9-3)[\[284\]](#refnote-spacexflight9) and experienced a fuel leak, resulting in a loss of control. The ship was [passivated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passivation_(spacecraft) "Passivation (spacecraft)") before reentry and broke up over the Indian Ocean.[&#8202;](#ref-back-F9-1)[\[287\]](#refnote-F9) |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| 10 | August 26, 2025<br>23:30:00[&#8202;](#ref-back-FT-0)[\[288\]](#refnote-FT) | [Block 2](#Block_2)<br>[B16](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Super_Heavy_boosters#B16 "List of Super Heavy boosters")[&#8202;](#ref-back-293-0)[\[289\]](#refnote-293) | [Block 2](#Block_2)<br>[S37](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Starship_vehicles#Block_2_launches_(S33-S38) "List of Starship vehicles")[&#8202;](#ref-back-TB-0)[\[290\]](#refnote-TB) | [Starbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starbase "SpaceX Starbase"), [OLP‑1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_Launch_Pad_1 "Orbital Launch Pad 1") | 8 [Starlink](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starlink "Starlink") simulator satellites[&#8202;](#ref-back-FT-1)[\[288\]](#refnote-FT) | ~16,000kg (35,000lb)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FT-2)[\[288\]](#refnote-FT) | [Transatmospheric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transatmospheric_orbit "Transatmospheric orbit") | [SpaceX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX "SpaceX") | Success | Controlled (gulf) | Controlled (ocean) |
| [Flight 10](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_10 "Starship flight test 10") was delayed by around two months[&#8202;](#ref-back-295-0)[\[291\]](#refnote-295) because the ship originally designated for the flight was lost during testing.[&#8202;](#ref-back-296-0)[\[292\]](#refnote-296) The booster ignited all thirty-three engines, though it lost one during the ascent burn. It would continue to complete its mission, splashing down in the Gulf of Mexico after simulating an engine out.[&#8202;](#ref-back-TA-0)[\[293\]](#refnote-TA) The ship reached the desired trajectory and deployed all eight of its Starlink simulators.[&#8202;](#ref-back-TA-1)[\[293\]](#refnote-TA) It then relit a single Raptor engine in space, followed by atmospheric entry.[&#8202;](#ref-back-TA-2)[\[293\]](#refnote-TA) During descent through the atmosphere, there was substantial damage to the engine section.[&#8202;](#ref-back-TA-3)[\[293\]](#refnote-TA) Despite this, S37 was able to softly splash down within three meters of its target site in the Indian Ocean.[&#8202;](#ref-back-TA-4)[\[293\]](#refnote-TA) |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| 11 | October 13, 2025<br>23:23:00[&#8202;](#ref-back-:5-0)[\[294\]](#refnote-:5) | [Block 2](#Block_2)<br>[B15‑2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Heavy_Booster_15 "Super Heavy Booster 15")[&#8202;](#ref-back-TB-1)[\[290\]](#refnote-TB) | [Block 2](#Block_2)<br>[S38](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Starship_vehicles#Block_2_launches_(S33-S38) "List of Starship vehicles")[&#8202;](#ref-back-TB-2)[\[290\]](#refnote-TB) | [Starbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starbase "SpaceX Starbase"), [OLP‑1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_Launch_Pad_1 "Orbital Launch Pad 1") | 8 Starlink simulator satellites[&#8202;](#ref-back-:1-0)[\[295\]](#refnote-:1) | ~16,000kg (35,000lb)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:1-1)[\[295\]](#refnote-:1) | [Transatmospheric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transatmospheric_orbit "Transatmospheric orbit") | [SpaceX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX "SpaceX") | Success | Controlled (gulf) | Controlled (ocean) |
| [Flight 11](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_11 "Starship flight test 11") was the last flight of Block 2 vehicles,[&#8202;](#ref-back-FT-3)[\[288\]](#refnote-FT) as well as the last flight from Pad-1 before its retrofit.[&#8202;](#ref-back-300-0)[\[296\]](#refnote-300) It flew a similar profile to the previous two flights,[&#8202;](#ref-back-:3-0)[\[297\]](#refnote-:3) with twenty-four engines flying for a second time on Booster 15.[&#8202;](#ref-back-302-0)[\[298\]](#refnote-302) The booster performed nominally during its flight, with the only anomaly being the loss of a Raptor engine on the boostback burn, though it would later reignite on the landing burn.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:5-1)[\[294\]](#refnote-:5) The ship, like on the previous flight, made it to SECO before deploying its eight Starlink simulators.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:5-2)[\[294\]](#refnote-:5) Following this, a single Raptor engine was lit in space, with the ship reentering shortly afterwards.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:5-3)[\[294\]](#refnote-:5) Unlike on Flight 10, the ship was mostly undamaged from heat on reentry, despite the intentional removal of several tiles.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:5-4)[\[294\]](#refnote-:5) S38 landed on target in the Indian Ocean.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:5-5)[\[294\]](#refnote-:5) |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
