| [Flight 12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_12 "Starship flight test 12") was originally intended to feature Booster 18 and Ship 39; however, Booster 18 was scrapped after sustaining severe damage during testing.[&#8202;](#ref-back-308-0)[\[304\]](#refnote-308) This mission marked the debut of the "Block 3" vehicle iteration and the inaugural use of Starbase's second launch pad.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:7-0)[\[305\]](#refnote-:7) It aimed for the controlled splashdown of both the Booster and the Ship.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:7-1)[\[305\]](#refnote-:7) The mission followed a flight profile similar to previous tests, with the ship targeting a trajectory just shy of orbit.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:3-1)[\[297\]](#refnote-:3) During ascent, the booster performed nominally despite losing a single outboard Raptor engine.[&#8202;](#ref-back-310-0)[\[306\]](#refnote-310) However, following hot-staging, Booster 19 only successfully relit 20 of the 33 engines intended for the boostback burn, the majority of which failed during startup.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:7-2)[\[305\]](#refnote-:7) The burn was then aborted after only a few seconds.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:7-3)[\[305\]](#refnote-:7) During the landing sequence, only one engine relit, leading the vehicle to impact the water at high speed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:7-4)[\[305\]](#refnote-:7) Ship 39 suffered the loss of one Raptor Vacuum engine during its ascent, but the vehicle's engine-out capability allowed it to successfully reach its scheduled engine cutoff (SECO).[&#8202;](#ref-back-:7-5)[\[305\]](#refnote-:7)[&#8202;](#ref-back-v3spacenews-0)[\[307\]](#refnote-v3spacenews) While the ship successfully deployed 20 satellite mass simulators and two modified functional Starlink satellites, the planned in-space Raptor relight was skipped.[&#8202;](#ref-back-312-0)[\[308\]](#refnote-312) The two functional satellites were used to test upcoming Starlink V3 hardware, capture and relay footage of the Starship in space, and inspect its heat shield.[&#8202;](#ref-back-v3spacenews-1)[\[307\]](#refnote-v3spacenews)[&#8202;](#ref-back-313-0)[\[309\]](#refnote-313) Finally, the ship completed a nominal reentry and a successful landing flip using two engines as planned.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:7-6)[\[305\]](#refnote-:7) |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| 13 | July 24, 2026<br>22:51:00[&#8202;](#ref-back-:8-0)[\[310\]](#refnote-:8) | [Block 3](#Block_3)<br>[B20](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Super_Heavy_boosters#B20 "List of Super Heavy boosters") | [Block 3](#Block_3)<br>[S40](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Starship_vehicles#Ship_40 "List of Starship vehicles") | [Starbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starbase "SpaceX Starbase"), [OLP‑2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_Launch_Pad_2 "Orbital Launch Pad 2") | 20 Starlink V3[&#8202;](#ref-back-:8-1)[\[310\]](#refnote-:8) | ~34,000kg (75,000lb)[&#8202;](#ref-back-flt12extrapolation-0)[\[e\]](#refnote-flt12extrapolation)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:8-2)[\[310\]](#refnote-:8)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:6-1)[\[303\]](#refnote-:6) | [Transatmospheric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transatmospheric_orbit "Transatmospheric orbit") | [SpaceX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX "SpaceX") | Success | Failure (gulf) | Controlled (ocean) |
| [Flight 13](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_13 "Starship flight test 13") followed a flight profile similar to previous tests. Liftoff, hot staging and boostback were nominal.[&#8202;](#ref-back-316-0)[\[311\]](#refnote-316) During the attempted landing burn, Booster 20 only lit 10 of the planned 13 engines on the landing burn and after losing five more, impacted the water at high speed. Flight 13 featured the first deployment of Starlink V3 satellites, which followed the trajectory of the ship and, after testing, burned up in the atmosphere as planned. After its simulated landing over the Indian Ocean, the ship splashed down and tipped over as planned. For the first time, the ship survived this, allowing SpaceX to get more images of the heat shield tiles, flaps and engine bay afterwards.[&#8202;](#ref-back-317-0)[\[312\]](#refnote-317) |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| 14 | September 28, 2026<br>12:48[&#8202;](#ref-back-NPR_Flight_14-0)[\[313\]](#refnote-NPR_Flight_14) | [Block 3](#Block_3)<br>[B21](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Super_Heavy_boosters#B21 "List of Super Heavy boosters") | [Block 3](#Block_3)<br>[S41](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Starship_vehicles#Ship_41 "List of Starship vehicles") | [Starbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starbase "SpaceX Starbase"), [OLP‑2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_Launch_Pad_2 "Orbital Launch Pad 2") | [Starlink](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starlink "Starlink"): [Group31-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Starlink_and_Starshield_launches#G31-1 "List of Starlink and Starshield launches")[&#8202;](#ref-back-Reuters_Flight_14-0)[\[314\]](#refnote-Reuters_Flight_14) | ~44,000kg (97,000lb)[&#8202;](#ref-back-flt12extrapolation-1)[\[e\]](#refnote-flt12extrapolation)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:8-3)[\[310\]](#refnote-:8)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:6-2)[\[303\]](#refnote-:6) | [LEO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Low_Earth_orbit "Low Earth orbit") | [SpaceX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX "SpaceX") | Success | Controlled (gulf) | Controlled (ocean) |
| [Flight 14](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_14 "Starship flight test 14") was Starship's first orbital mission and first deployment of an operational payload. Booster 21 completed ascent, hot-staging and boostback before making a controlled splashdown in the Gulf of Mexico, despite several engines shutting down during descent. During ascent, one of Ship 41's engines shut down prematurely, but flight controllers proceeded with orbital insertion. Ship 41 reached an orbit at an altitude of approximately 275 kilometres (171mi),[&#8202;](#ref-back-Reuters_Flight_14-1)[\[314\]](#refnote-Reuters_Flight_14)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Gizmodo_Flight_14-0)[\[315\]](#refnote-Gizmodo_Flight_14) deployed all 26 Starlink V3 satellites,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Reuters_Flight_14-2)[\[314\]](#refnote-Reuters_Flight_14) and made a controlled splashdown in the Pacific Ocean at about 15:57 UTC, roughly three hours after launch.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Gizmodo_Flight_14-1)[\[315\]](#refnote-Gizmodo_Flight_14) The planned six-orbit mission was shortened to just under two orbits following payload deployment and an initial health check, foregoing the planned coast period.[&#8202;](#ref-back-321-0)[\[316\]](#refnote-321) |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |

This section is [transcluded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:Transclusion "Help:Transclusion") from [List of Starship launches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Starship_launches "List of Starship launches"). | In April 2023, the [Luxembourg Space Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luxembourg_Space_Agency "Luxembourg Space Agency") (LSA) and OffWorld Europe announced a partnership to develop a system for extracting, processing, storing, and using water ice from the lunar surface through [in situ resource utilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/In_situ_resource_utilization "In situ resource utilization"). Overseen by the [European Space Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Space_Agency "European Space Agency"), the project combines LSA's and OffWorld Europe's expertise, with a technology demonstration planned for launch to the Moon in 2027 aboard the first Starship HLS mission for the Artemis program.[&#8202;](#ref-back-OffWorld_2023-2)[\[337\]](#refnote-OffWorld_2023) The mission is expected to require an estimated high-teens number of Starship refueling flights beforehand.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Smith_2023-0)[\[338\]](#refnote-Smith_2023) |  |  |  |  |  |  |

## Potential missions

### Starlink

SpaceX plans to use Starship to launch the second generation of satellites for SpaceX's [Starlink](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starlink "Starlink") system, which as of 2021 delivered high-speed internet to over 70 countries.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Sheetz-2021b-0)[\[358\]](#refnote-Sheetz-2021b) An analyst at financial services company [Morgan Stanley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morgan_Stanley "Morgan Stanley") stated development of Starship and Starlink are intertwined, with Starship's planned launch capacity enabling cheaper Starlink launches, and Starlink's profits financing Starship's development costs.[&#8202;](#ref-back-364-0)[\[359\]](#refnote-364) In deficit from its inception until the end of 2022,[&#8202;](#ref-back-365-0)[\[360\]](#refnote-365) Starlink was first reported to be cash flow positive in the first quarter of 2023,[&#8202;](#ref-back-366-0)[\[361\]](#refnote-366) though Elon Musk said that Starlink had only reached "break-even cashflow" in 2023.[&#8202;](#ref-back-367-0)[\[362\]](#refnote-367) In December 2023, the [FCC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FCC "FCC") issued a final denial of a $885million Starlink [RDOF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_Service_Fund#Connect_America_Fund "Universal Service Fund") subsidy because of Starlink's "continuing inability to successfully launch on the Starship rocket".[&#8202;](#ref-back-fccdenial-0)[\[363\]](#refnote-fccdenial)

### Artemis Program

[![See caption and article](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c1/Realistic_Artemis_3_mission_diagram_labeled.png/960px-Realistic_Artemis_3_mission_diagram_labeled.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Realistic_Artemis_3_mission_diagram_labeled.png) [Artemis III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artemis_III "Artemis III") launch profile of a human landing on the Moon, involving Starship HLS, Starship tanker variants, and [Orion spacecraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orion_(spacecraft) "Orion (spacecraft)")

[Starship HLS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_HLS "Starship HLS") was initially chosen by NASA as the sole lunar [Human Landing System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Landing_System "Human Landing System") for the planned [Artemis III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artemis_III "Artemis III") and [Artemis IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artemis_IV "Artemis IV") In February 2026, NASA restructured the program: [Artemis III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artemis_III "Artemis III") became a mid-2027 crewed test in low Earth orbit, in which [Orion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orion_(spacecraft) "Orion (spacecraft)") docks with Starship and/or Blue Origin's lander, and [Artemis IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artemis_IV "Artemis IV") became the first landing attempt, targeted for 2028.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Ars-Berger-Artemis-1)[\[15\]](#refnote-Ars-Berger-Artemis)[&#8202;](#ref-back-369-0)[\[364\]](#refnote-369) Starship HLS is to be launched into a low Earth orbit, and refueled by multiple Starship tanker spacecraft.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NASA_IG-0)[\[365\]](#refnote-NASA_IG):4,5 Once fueled, it would perform a trans lunar injection burn and enter a [near-rectilinear halo orbit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Near-rectilinear_halo_orbit "Near-rectilinear halo orbit")[&#8202;](#ref-back-Gerstenmaier-0)[\[366\]](#refnote-Gerstenmaier) around the Moon, with a [perilune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perilune "Perilune") of 1,500km (930mi) occurring over the north pole and an [apolune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apolune "Apolune") of 70,000km (43,000mi) occurring over the south pole.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NASA_IG-1)[\[365\]](#refnote-NASA_IG):4,5 The [Orion spacecraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orion_(spacecraft) "Orion (spacecraft)") would then dock with Starship HLS and two of its four crew would transfer into Starship HLS.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NASA_IG-2)[\[365\]](#refnote-NASA_IG):4,5 Starship HLS would then use its engines to make a powered descent and land near the [lunar south pole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_south_pole "Lunar south pole").[&#8202;](#ref-back-NASA_IG-3)[\[365\]](#refnote-NASA_IG):4,5 After the crew performs the surface portion of its mission, the HLS would ascend with the crew.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NASA_IG-4)[\[365\]](#refnote-NASA_IG):4,5 The crew would then transfer into the Orion spacecraft and return to Earth.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NASA_IG-5)[\[365\]](#refnote-NASA_IG):4,5

### Astronomy

Astronomers have called to consider Starship's larger mass to orbit and wider cargo bay for proposed space telescopes such as [LUVOIR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Large_Ultraviolet_Optical_Infrared_Surveyor "Large Ultraviolet Optical Infrared Surveyor"), and to develop larger telescopes to take advantage of these capabilities.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Elvis-0)[\[367\]](#refnote-Elvis) Starship's 9m (30ft) fairing width could hold an 8m (26ft) wide space telescope mirror in a single piece,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Clark-0)[\[368\]](#refnote-Clark) alleviating the need for complex unfolding such as that of the [JWST](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Webb_Space_Telescope "James Webb Space Telescope")'s 6.5m (21ft) mirror, which added cost and delays.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Elvis-1)[\[367\]](#refnote-Elvis)

Regarding larger mass to orbit limits, the [Ariane 5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ariane_5 "Ariane 5") imposed a ~6,500kg limit on the telescope's weight.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Kuhr_2024-0)[\[369\]](#refnote-Kuhr_2024) Starship's low launch cost could also allow probes to use heavier, more common, less expensive materials, such as glass instead of [beryllium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beryllium "Beryllium") for large telescope mirrors.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Elvis-2)[\[367\]](#refnote-Elvis) With a 5t (11,000lb) mirror built using similar methods to the [Hubble Space Telescope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hubble_Space_Telescope "Hubble Space Telescope")'s mirror, the JWST would represent only 10% of the mass deliverable by a (refueled) Starship to the Sun–Earth [L2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lagrange_point "Lagrange point") point, and therefore minimizing the weight of the telescope would not have been a dominant design consideration.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Elvis-3)[\[367\]](#refnote-Elvis)

The [National Academies of Science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Academy_of_Sciences "National Academy of Sciences")'s [2020 survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astronomy_and_Astrophysics_Decadal_Survey "Astronomy and Astrophysics Decadal Survey") recommended the [Habitable Worlds Observatory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habitable_Worlds_Observatory "Habitable Worlds Observatory") (HWO); the space observatory, requiring a super heavy lift launch vehicle, will search for [signs of life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biosignature "Biosignature") on [exoplanets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exoplanet "Exoplanet").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Kuhr_2024-1)[\[369\]](#refnote-Kuhr_2024) The HWO's team hopes for the success of big launchers due to their critical importance to the HWO's mission.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Kuhr_2024-2)[\[369\]](#refnote-Kuhr_2024) Lee Feinberg, NASA HWO lead architect[&#8202;](#ref-back-Kuhr_2024-3)[\[369\]](#refnote-Kuhr_2024) and JWST manager,[&#8202;](#ref-back-375-0)[\[370\]](#refnote-375) stays in communication with SpaceX to track Starship's progress and has visited them in 2024 for that purpose.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Kuhr_2024-4)[\[369\]](#refnote-Kuhr_2024) The NASA Habitable Worlds Observatory will have a 6–8 meter mirror for now, but its design should be flexible to leverage launchers with potentially double the mass and volume by the time it launches in the 2040s.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Kuhr_2024-5)[\[369\]](#refnote-Kuhr_2024) Former NASA [JPL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jet_Propulsion_Laboratory "Jet Propulsion Laboratory") architect Casey Handmer believes the HWO to be far too conservative compared to what is possible with Starship.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Kuhr_2024-6)[\[369\]](#refnote-Kuhr_2024) Handmer argues that Starship enables telescopes to scale up to the point of surface-level [exoplanet imaging](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_directly_imaged_exoplanets "List of directly imaged exoplanets"), perhaps big enough to detect seasonal [migration patterns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Migration_patterns "Migration patterns").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Kuhr_2024-7)[\[369\]](#refnote-Kuhr_2024)

### Rocket cargo

Main article: [Rocket Cargo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rocket_Cargo "Rocket Cargo")

In January 2022, SpaceX was awarded a $102million five-year contract to develop the [Rocket Cargo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rocket_Cargo "Rocket Cargo") program for the [United States Space Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Space_Force "United States Space Force").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Erwin-1)[\[244\]](#refnote-Erwin) The five-year contract is intended to "determine exactly what a rocket can achieve when used for cargo transport",[&#8202;](#ref-back-AeroTime-0)[\[371\]](#refnote-AeroTime) and will see the [Air Force Research Laboratory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_Force_Research_Laboratory "Air Force Research Laboratory") collect data during commercial launches of Starship.[&#8202;](#ref-back-AeroTime-1)[\[371\]](#refnote-AeroTime) The contract includes an eventual demonstration mission with the launch and landing of a cargo-laden Starship in a point-to-point flight.

The [Department of Defense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Defense "United States Department of Defense") has planned a test with Starship as part of its program to demonstrate the ability to rapidly deploy up to 100tons of cargo and supplies, a capability it calls point-to-point delivery (P2PD). The test was envisioned to take place in FY25 or FY26.[&#8202;](#ref-back-377-0)[\[372\]](#refnote-377)

### Mars Sample Return

In 2024, the [NASA-ESA Mars Sample Return](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NASA-ESA_Mars_Sample_Return "NASA-ESA Mars Sample Return") project, one of NASA's highest priority flagship projects, suffered a setback when an independent review board assessing the project's feasibility concluded that the project could not be completed under its mission profile. In April 2024, the [Administrator of NASA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administrator_of_NASA "Administrator of NASA") then announced that a new mission profile was needed for the project and that NASA would turn to industry for proposals, with responses due in fall 2024, and a high emphasis on lower total cost and lower risk.[&#8202;](#ref-back-378-0)[\[373\]](#refnote-378) Starship was proposed by some space journalists as a leading candidate to serve as a central component of the new mission profile architecture.[&#8202;](#ref-back-379-0)[\[374\]](#refnote-379)

### Transportation

SpaceX has proposed using Starship for [point-to-point flights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spaceflight#Point-to-point "Spaceflight") (called "Earth to Earth" flights by SpaceX), traveling anywhere on Earth in under an hour.[&#8202;](#ref-back-380-0)[\[375\]](#refnote-380) Musk stated that SpaceX would complete hundreds of cargo flights before launching with human passengers.[&#8202;](#ref-back-cnbc20200901-0)[\[376\]](#refnote-cnbc20200901)

### Space colonization

Further information: [SpaceX Mars colonization program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Mars_colonization_program "SpaceX Mars colonization program")

According to SpaceX, the design of Starship is driven by its requirement to be able to land crews on Mars,[&#8202;](#ref-back-382-0)[\[377\]](#refnote-382):120 though SpaceX has not published technical plans or designs about Starship's [life support systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life-support_system "Life-support system"), [radiation protection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radiation_protection "Radiation protection"), docking system, or in-orbit refueling system for Mars.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Grush-0)[\[378\]](#refnote-Grush) The spacecraft would be launched to low Earth orbit and refueled in orbit before heading to Mars.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Pearson-0)[\[379\]](#refnote-Pearson) After landing on Mars, the [Sabatier reaction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabatier_reaction "Sabatier reaction") could be used to synthesize liquid methane and liquid oxygen, Starship's fuel, in a [power-to-gas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Power-to-gas "Power-to-gas") plant.[&#8202;](#ref-back-385-0)[\[380\]](#refnote-385) The plant's [raw resources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/In_situ_resource_utilization "In situ resource utilization") would be [Martian water](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_on_Mars "Water on Mars") and [Martian carbon dioxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atmosphere_of_Mars#Carbon_dioxide "Atmosphere of Mars").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Sommerlad-1)[\[98\]](#refnote-Sommerlad) On Earth, similar technologies could be used to make [carbon-neutral propellant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon-neutral_fuel "Carbon-neutral fuel") for the rocket.[&#8202;](#ref-back-386-0)[\[381\]](#refnote-386) To date, there has been one proof of concept experiment ( [MOXIE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MOXIE "MOXIE")) demonstrating the extraction of oxygen from Martian carbon dioxide, with [George Dvorsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Dvorsky "George Dvorsky") writing for [Gizmodo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gizmodo "Gizmodo") commenting that we are not "remotely close" to turning this "into something practical".[&#8202;](#ref-back-Gizmodo-0)[\[382\]](#refnote-Gizmodo)

SpaceX and Musk have stated [their goal of colonizing Mars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Mars_colonization_program "SpaceX Mars colonization program") to ensure the [long-term survival of humanity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_and_survival "Space and survival"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-NYT-0)[\[383\]](#refnote-NYT) with an ambition of having sent one million people to Mars by 2050.[&#8202;](#ref-back-389-0)[\[384\]](#refnote-389) In March 2022, he estimated that the first crewed Mars landing could occur in 2029.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Torchinsky-0)[\[385\]](#refnote-Torchinsky) This timeline has been criticized as unrealistic by Kevin Olsen, a physicist at the [University of Oxford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Oxford "University of Oxford"), England, who has said that "colony needs to become a factory" to produce air, fuel and water as it is "fundamentally impossible to create a completely closed environment in space", and that the technology to do so is "far, far behind the technology of space flight and habitation construction".[&#8202;](#ref-back-Gizmodo-1)[\[382\]](#refnote-Gizmodo) Serkan Saydam, a mining engineering professor from the [University of New South Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_New_South_Wales "University of New South Wales"), Australia, stated that humanity currently lacks the necessary technology to establish a Martian colony, and will likely lack the capacity to establish a Martian city with one million people by 2050.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Gizmodo-2)[\[382\]](#refnote-Gizmodo)

### Other missions

A future payload is the [Superbird-9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Superbird-9 "Superbird-9") communication satellite, which was Starship's first contract for externally made commercial satellites.[&#8202;](#ref-back-391-0)[\[386\]](#refnote-391) Another planned payload is the [Starlab space station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starlab_(space_station) "Starlab (space station)"), which Starship expects to launch in a single piece.[&#8202;](#ref-back-spacecom-0)[\[387\]](#refnote-spacecom)

In the future, the spacecraft's crewed version could be used for [space tourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_tourism "Space tourism")—for example, for the third flight of the [Polaris program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polaris_program "Polaris program").[&#8202;](#ref-back-393-0)[\[388\]](#refnote-393)

Research conducted by [Project Lyra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Project_Lyra "Project Lyra") determined that with refueling in LEO, a Starship could send a spacecraft to [ʻOumuamua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%CA%BBOumuamua "ʻOumuamua") on a journey taking 20 years.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Hibberd_2023-0)[\[389\]](#refnote-Hibberd_2023) A gravity assist would be required at Jupiter.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Hibberd_2023-1)[\[389\]](#refnote-Hibberd_2023)

## Facilities

Further information: [SpaceX Starbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starbase "SpaceX Starbase") and [SpaceX facilities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_facilities "SpaceX facilities")

### Testing and manufacturing

[![Various spacecraft constructed inside bays](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7a/Bays_%2B_ships.jpg/500px-Bays_%2B_ships.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Bays_+_ships.jpg) [Ship 27](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_Ship_27 "Starship Ship 27"), [Ship 26](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_Ship_26 "Starship Ship 26") and [Booster 10](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Heavy_Booster_10 "Super Heavy Booster 10") forward section under construction in Starbase build site, March 2023

[Starbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starbase "SpaceX Starbase") consists of a manufacturing facility and launch site,[&#8202;](#ref-back-395-0)[\[390\]](#refnote-395) and is located at Boca Chica, Texas. Both facilities operate 24 hours a day.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Berger-2020b-2)[\[27\]](#refnote-Berger-2020b) A maximum of 450 full-time employees may be onsite.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FAA-2)[\[111\]](#refnote-FAA):28 The site consists of two launch sites, one payload processing facility, one seven-acre (2.8ha) [solar farm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Photovoltaic_power_station "Photovoltaic power station"), and other facilities.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FAA-3)[\[111\]](#refnote-FAA):34–36

Raptor engines are tested at the [Rocket Development facility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_McGregor "SpaceX McGregor") in McGregor, Texas. The facility has two main test stands: one horizontal stand for both engine types and one vertical stand for sea-level-optimized rocket engines.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Davenport-1)[\[391\]](#refnote-Davenport) In the future, a nearby factory, which as of September2021[\[update\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=SpaceX_Starship&action=edit) was under construction, will make the new generation of sea-level Raptors while SpaceX's headquarters in California will continue building the Raptor Vacuum and test new designs.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Davenport-2)[\[391\]](#refnote-Davenport)

At Florida, a facility at Cocoa purifies [silica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silicon_dioxide "Silicon dioxide") for Starship heat-shield tiles, producing a [slurry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slurry "Slurry") that is then shipped to a facility at Cape Canaveral. In the past, workers constructed the [Starship MK2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_MK2 "Starship MK2") prototype in competition with Starbase's crews.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Bergeron-1)[\[56\]](#refnote-Bergeron-2021) The [Kennedy Space Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kennedy_Space_Center "Kennedy Space Center"), also in Florida, is planned to host other Starship facilities, such as a Starship launch site at [Launch Complex 39A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kennedy_Space_Center_Launch_Complex_39A "Kennedy Space Center Launch Complex 39A") and a production facility at Roberts Road. This production facility is being expanded from "Hangar X", the Falcon rocket boosters' storage and maintenance facility. It will include a 30,000m2 (320,000ft2) building, [loading dock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loading_dock "Loading dock"), and a place for constructing integration tower sections.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Bergin-2)[\[392\]](#refnote-Bergin) Adjacent to the Kennedy Space Center will be an additional launch site at [Cape Canaveral Space Launch Complex 37](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Canaveral_Space_Launch_Complex_37 "Cape Canaveral Space Launch Complex 37"), likely to service missions for the complex owner, the [United States Space Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Space_Force "United States Space Force").

### Launch sites

#### Starbase

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e9/Starbase_OLM_construction_08-2021.jpg/500px-Starbase_OLM_construction_08-2021.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Starbase_OLM_construction_08-2021.jpg) Orbital launch mount A under construction in Starbase, August 2021

Starbase hosts two launch sites, named Pad A and Pad B or Orbital Launch Pads 1 and 2 (OLP-1 and 2).[&#8202;](#ref-back-FAA-4)[\[111\]](#refnote-FAA):34 A launch site at Starbase has large facilities, such as a [tank farm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_terminal "Oil terminal"), an orbital launch mount, and an integration tower.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FAA-5)[\[111\]](#refnote-FAA) Smaller facilities are present at the launch site: tanks surrounding the area containing methane, oxygen, nitrogen, helium, [hydraulic fluid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydraulic_fluid "Hydraulic fluid"), etc.;[&#8202;](#ref-back-FAA-6)[\[111\]](#refnote-FAA):161[subcoolers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subcooling#In_spaceflight "Subcooling") near the tank farm cool propellant using liquid nitrogen; and various pipes are installed at large facilities.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Weber-4)[\[112\]](#refnote-Weber) Each tank farm consists of eight tanks, enough to support one orbital launch.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Weber-5)[\[112\]](#refnote-Weber) The current\[ _[when?](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Manual_of_Style/Dates_and_numbers#Chronological_items "Wikipedia:Manual of Style/Dates and numbers")_\] launch mount on Pad A has a water-powered [flame diverter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flame_deflector "Flame deflector"), 20 clamps holding the booster, and a quick disconnect mount providing liquid fuel and electricity to the Super Heavy booster before it lifts off.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Weber-6)[\[112\]](#refnote-Weber)

The integration tower or [launch tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Service_structure "Service structure") consists of [steel truss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steel_truss "Steel truss") sections, a [lightning rod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lightning_rod "Lightning rod") on top,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Berger-2021a-0)[\[393\]](#refnote-Berger-2021a) and a pair of mechanical arms that can lift, catch and recover the booster.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Weber-7)[\[112\]](#refnote-Weber) The decision to catch the booster with the arms was made to reduce the rocket's mass and mechanical complexity by removing the need for landing legs, as well as enabling more rapid reuse by placing the rocket directly back on the launchpad.[&#8202;](#ref-back-SpaceX-2023a-1)[\[165\]](#refnote-SpaceX-2023a):2 The mechanical arms are attached to a carriage and controlled by a [pulley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulley "Pulley") at the top of the tower.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Weber-8)[\[112\]](#refnote-Weber) The pulley is linked to a [winch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winch "Winch") and [spool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cable_reel "Cable reel") at the base of the tower using a cable.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Weber-9)[\[112\]](#refnote-Weber) Using the winch and the carriage, the mechanical arms can move vertically, with support from [bearings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bearing_(mechanical) "Bearing (mechanical)") attached at the sides of the carriage.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Weber-10)[\[112\]](#refnote-Weber) A linear [hydraulic actuator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydraulic_cylinder "Hydraulic cylinder") moves the arms horizontally. On top of the arms are tracks, which are used to position the booster or spacecraft.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Weber-11)[\[112\]](#refnote-Weber) The tower is mounted with a quick disconnect arm extending to and contracting from the Starship spacecraft; its functions are similar to the quick disconnect mount that powers the booster.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Weber-12)[\[112\]](#refnote-Weber)

#### Florida

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ca/Axiom-3_Launch_%28KSC-20240118-PH-CMS01_0022%29_Cropped.jpg/960px-Axiom-3_Launch_%28KSC-20240118-PH-CMS01_0022%29_Cropped.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Axiom-3_Launch_(KSC-20240118-PH-CMS01_0022)_Cropped.jpg) Starship launch tower construction can be seen (right) at LC-39A in January 2024 as Falcon 9 launches continue to take place

SpaceX has been constructing a Starship launch pad at [Kennedy Space Center Launch Complex 39A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kennedy_Space_Center_Launch_Complex_39A "Kennedy Space Center Launch Complex 39A") (LC-39A) since 2021. The site was leased to the company in 2014 and is used to launch [Falcon 9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_9 "Falcon 9") rockets.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Bergin-3)[\[392\]](#refnote-Bergin)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Roulette-3)[\[394\]](#refnote-Roulette) A [Finding of No Significant Impact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finding_of_No_Significant_Impact "Finding of No Significant Impact") was issued by SpaceX's [environmental impact statement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_impact_statement "Environmental impact statement") (EIS), with NASA as the lead agency, earlier in September 2019 for the launch pad site.[&#8202;](#ref-back-400-0)[\[395\]](#refnote-400) In 2024, the [Federal Aviation Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Aviation_Administration "Federal Aviation Administration") began the process of preparing an EIS evaluating the potential impacts of the new infrastructure and a higher launch cadence of up to 44 per year at LC-39A.[&#8202;](#ref-back-401-0)[\[396\]](#refnote-401)

In June 2024, Blue Origin and [United Launch Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Launch_Alliance "United Launch Alliance") (ULA) provided comments as part of the EIS process, both objecting to the impact that Starship launch operations may have on their own activities at the site.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Clark_2024-1)[\[322\]](#refnote-Clark_2024) Blue Origin suggested several mitigations, including allowing other operators to object to a Starship launch that would conflict with one of its own, limiting Starship operations to particular times, or expanding the number of launchpads in the area to reduce the impact of conflicting launches.[&#8202;](#ref-back-402-0)[\[397\]](#refnote-402) ULA suggested regulators prevent Starship from launching in Florida altogether because a fully fueled Starship would require an evacuation zone so large that it would prevent other operators from using their facilities, and the noise generated by repetitive launches could be injurious to those who live or work nearby.[&#8202;](#ref-back-403-0)[\[398\]](#refnote-403) Elon Musk suggested that the two companies' comments were disingenuous and that their true motivation was to impede SpaceX's progress by [lawfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lawfare "Lawfare").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Clark_2024-2)[\[322\]](#refnote-Clark_2024)

The company has also proposed building another Starship launch pad at the nearby [Cape Canaveral Space Launch Complex 37](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Canaveral_Space_Launch_Complex_37 "Cape Canaveral Space Launch Complex 37") (SLC-37) which became vacant in 2024 after the retirement of the [Delta IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delta_IV "Delta IV") rocket. That year, the [United States Space Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Space_Force "United States Space Force") began the process of preparing an EIS evaluating the potential impacts of new infrastructure and a launch cadence of up to 76 times per year at SLC-37.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Clark-1)[\[399\]](#refnote-Clark) SpaceX and NASA have also worked on assessing LC-49 to the north of 39A.[&#8202;](#ref-back-405-0)[\[400\]](#refnote-405) The Kennedy Space Center's Master Plan One has stated that LC-49 could avoid overflight issues with pad 39B and minimize conflict with the [Canaveral National Seashore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canaveral_National_Seashore "Canaveral National Seashore").[&#8202;](#ref-back-406-0)[\[401\]](#refnote-406) As of January 18, 2024, NASA did not have activities underway at LC-49.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Nasa_2024-0)[\[402\]](#refnote-Nasa_2024)

Both EIS processes must be complete before SpaceX will be cleared to launch Starship from Florida, which likely will not occur until late 2025.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Clark_2024-3)[\[322\]](#refnote-Clark_2024) The [Playalinda Beach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Playalinda_Beach "Playalinda Beach") has been closed by KSC Police and the National Park Service for many launches from 39A and 39B.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Nasa_2024-1)[\[402\]](#refnote-Nasa_2024) The towers and mechanical arms at the sites should be similar to the ones at Starbase.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Bergin-4)[\[392\]](#refnote-Bergin)

## Responses to Starship development

Further information: [SpaceX Starbase §Impact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starbase#Impact "SpaceX Starbase")

In order to compete with SpaceX and close their technological gap with the company, the [China Aerospace Science and Tech Corp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_Aerospace_Science_and_Technology_Corporation "China Aerospace Science and Technology Corporation") and other aerospace actors in China have reportedly been working on their own equivalent of Starship—the [Long March 9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long_March_9 "Long March 9") super-heavy lift rocket,[&#8202;](#ref-back-408-0)[\[403\]](#refnote-408) which is also designed to eventually be fully reusable.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NSF202304272-0)[\[404\]](#refnote-NSF202304272) In 2021, the [China Academy of Launch Vehicle Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_Academy_of_Launch_Vehicle_Technology "China Academy of Launch Vehicle Technology") (CALT) showed a rendered video of a rocket noted to be "strikingly" similar to Starship in appearance and function.[&#8202;](#ref-back-410-0)[\[405\]](#refnote-410) In a 2022 event organized by the [International Astronautical Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Astronautical_Federation "International Astronautical Federation") and the [Chinese Society of Astronautics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Society_of_Astronautics "Chinese Society of Astronautics"), the CALT communicated performing research on a crewed launch vehicle powered by LOX-methane propellant, with a second stage that was very similar to Starship's.[&#8202;](#ref-back-411-0)[\[406\]](#refnote-411)

_[SpaceNews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceNews "SpaceNews")_ noted that the Chinese start-up _Space Epoch_ and engine maker [Jiuzhou Yunjian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jiuzhou_Yunjian "Jiuzhou Yunjian") were developing a smaller Starship-like rocket with a methane-LOX engine similar to Raptor, stainless steel tanks, and an iterative design.[&#8202;](#ref-back-412-0)[\[407\]](#refnote-412) Starship's reusability and stainless-steel construction might also have inspired [Project Jarvis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Project_Jarvis "Project Jarvis"), a reusable upper stage for [Blue Origin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Origin "Blue Origin")'s [New Glenn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Glenn "New Glenn") heavy-lift launch vehicle intended to replace New Glenn's expendable upper stage in the future.[&#8202;](#ref-back-413-0)[\[408\]](#refnote-413)

In 2021, members of the U.S. Congress voiced concerns about the U.S. FAA's response to SpaceX's launch license violations following the explosion of SN8, calling on the FAA to "resist any potential undue influence on launch safety decision-making".[&#8202;](#ref-back-SpaceNews-0)[\[409\]](#refnote-SpaceNews) In 2023, prior to [Starship's second test flight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_2 "Starship flight test 2"), SpaceX's vice president and ex-NASA engineer [Bill Gerstenmaier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_H._Gerstenmaier "William H. Gerstenmaier") made statements at the U.S. Senate on the importance of innovation in light of "strategic competition from state actors like China".[&#8202;](#ref-back-Fisher-0)[\[410\]](#refnote-Fisher) He said SpaceX was under a contract with NASA to use Starship to land American astronauts on the Moon before China does,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Bloomberg-0)[\[411\]](#refnote-Bloomberg) and that the Starship test flights campaign was being held up by "regulatory headwinds and unnecessary bureaucracy" unrelated to public safety.[&#8202;](#ref-back-417-0)[\[412\]](#refnote-417)

Following the second flight test of Starship, the U.S. [Government Accountability Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_Accountability_Office "Government Accountability Office") (GAO) made recommendations to the U.S. FAA to "improve its mishap investigation process", finding that historically they have allowed the launch operator to conduct their investigation with the FAA supervising.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Jones-0)[\[413\]](#refnote-Jones) Several environmental groups have filed lawsuits against the FAA and SpaceX, claiming that environmental reviews were bypassed due to Musk's political and financial influence.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Fine-0)[\[414\]](#refnote-Fine)

## Notes

1. [&#8202;](#refnote-21)[↑](#ref-back-21-0)Super Heavy [dry mass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vehicle_weight "Vehicle weight"): 200t (440,000lb); Starship dry mass: 100t (220,000lb); Super Heavy propellant mass: 3,400t (7,500,000lb);[&#8202;](#ref-back-Sesnic-9)[\[19\]](#refnote-Sesnic) Starship propellant mass: 1,500t (3,300,000lb).[&#8202;](#ref-back-Lawler-0)[\[20\]](#refnote-Lawler) The total of these masses is about 5,300t (11,700,000lb).
2. [&#8202;](#refnote-35)[↑](#ref-back-35-0)With a mixture ratio of 3.6 parts oxygen to 1 part methane, 78.3% of 3650t is 2860t of liquid oxygen.
3. [&#8202;](#refnote-67)[↑](#ref-back-67-0)78% of 1,500t (3,300,000lb) is 1,170t (2,580,000lb) of liquid oxygen.
4. [&#8202;](#refnote-vehicle)[1](#ref-back-vehicle-0) [2](#ref-back-vehicle-1) [3](#ref-back-vehicle-2) [4](#ref-back-vehicle-3) [5](#ref-back-vehicle-4) [6](#ref-back-vehicle-5) [7](#ref-back-vehicle-6) [8](#ref-back-vehicle-7)Starship vehicles have a multiple-digit serial number, followed by a hyphen and a number that indicates the flight count. Starship vehicles are in a S#-# format, while boosters are B#-#. For example, B14‑1 and B14‑2 represent the first and second flights of B14. Vehicles without a hyphen were either expended on their first flight or retired after recovery. Missions where a new vehicle is used are marked with a mint-colored  background.
5. [&#8202;](#refnote-flt12extrapolation)[1](#ref-back-flt12extrapolation-0) [2](#ref-back-flt12extrapolation-1)Extrapolated from payload mass of flight 12.
